#140. Steve Rosenberg: Historic talks with Xi and Trump are a coup for Putin

Nearly five years after the full-scale invasion of Ukraine turned him into a pariah, Putin is back on the global stage

But Thursday's mini summit of Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping already looks like a win for the Kremlin.

Think back to 2022. After Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine President Putin was an international pariah. Western leaders were determined to isolate him and his country over the war in Ukraine.

Fast forward to 2026: Vladimir Putin has secured an historic meeting in Shenzhen. For the first time in history, the leaders of China, Russia and the United States will sit down â just the three of them â for talks.

That puts Putin exactly where he wants to be: at the top table of global politics, rubbing shoulders with the leaders of the two great powers.

It allows him to boast that Western pressure on Russia has failed.

"We see such examples in the failed attempts to isolate our country, Russia, which has faced unprecedented sanctions pressure," Putin told a business forum of the Asia-Pacific Economic Co-operation organisation.

That was followed by a meeting with Xi Jinping.

But the Kremlin says the "showstopper" event in Shenzhen will be Putin's trilateral with Xi and Trump.

It allows him to play global statesman, even as the Russian military continues to pound Ukrainian towns and cities, and the Kremlin continues to reject the idea of an unconditional, comprehensive ceasefire.

One short meeting is unlikely to reshape the world. But it's the optics that are key. The picture. The photo-op. Three leaders, three big powers, discussing big issues.

In a way, Vladimir Putin will use this meeting to send a holiday postcard to the rest of the world. Instead of "Wish you were here", he'll sign it: "Look: I'm here."

But what of those who are not at the table?

Among European leaders, and especially in Ukraine, there is concern that the three-way meeting might produce more than just a pretty picture, though not in a good way for Kyiv.

Donald Trump has already said that in Shenzhen he wants to discuss his "favourite subject" - the war in Ukraine. The Kremlin would likely welcome an opportunity to argue its case to Trump and Xi without Ukrainian officials or European leaders in the room.

Last month Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky expressed concern that the Putin-Trump-Xi meeting might resemble the Yalta Conference of 1945. That is when the leaders of the Soviet Union, Britain and the United States â Joseph Stalin, Winston Churchill and Franklin D Roosevelt - met to re-organise post-World War Two Europe.

"We really don't want this to turn into 'Yalta 2, 3, 4' with a redistribution of influence," said Zelensky.

It is unclear whether China would want the Ukraine war on the agenda in Shenzhen. In its official statements on the upcoming trilateral meeting, Beijing has talked more generally about the need to discuss "safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability".

In Shenzhen, Russia will make the most of the optics. But a diplomatic victory is not the same as a military one.

Nearly five years after President Putin launched his so-called "special military operation", Russia is still struggling to win its war on Ukraine.

Over this time, it has suffered huge losses on the battlefield.

And the Ukrainian strike on the road bridge linking Russia to the Crimean Peninsula â carried out on the eve of the summit - was a blow to Moscow's prestige. The attack severely damaged part of the multi-billion-dollar bridge built by Russia after it annexed Crimea from Ukraine.

The damage is not only a logistical headache for the Russian authorities.

It is a reminder that the Kremlin's "special operation" has not gone at all to plan.

The Shenzhen summit may give Vladimir Putin a big stage, and a bright spotlight.

It does not necessarily bring him closer to winning the war.

#139. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#137. India has been named a ‘destination of the year’ for 2027 – here are insiders’ tips on travelling beyond the tropes

As tourism booms, three experienced female travellers give their take on how to make the most of travelling here

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

The 13th-century Persian scholar Zakariyya al-Qazwini never travelled to India. But he catalogued it in his famous work The Wonders of Creation and Rarities of Existence, which drew on accounts of a distant land filled with “rarities par excellence”. For al-Qazwini, as Helen Pfeifer writes in the London Review of Books, wonder was not an emotion but an intellectual method of making sense of the world and acquiring knowledge.

India has long occupied such a space in the global imagination, as a land of extraordinary riches; spirituality, customs, chaos and contradictions. This has shaped how the country is perceived from afar, but it can often flatten its complexities.

So when, last week, Travel + Leisure named India its destination of the year for 2027, I found myself considering what it means that the country holds such multitudes. The magazine’s recognition will undoubtedly add to its growing appeal as a travel destination. But expectations built around India can differ from the experience of being there, particularly for women.

My own experiences of travelling in India largely surround my work as a journalist, often visiting places that would never make it into a travel guide. I remember the towering chinar tree outside the home of a woman in Kashmir whose son had been killed in an encounter with the security forces; the rolling hills of Odisha, where we travelled to meet a guerrilla fighter; the tiny hut in Chhattisgarh where, after a 13-hour journey, our team found a meal of rice and lentils. These scenes have stayed in my mind, for their landscapes as much as for the stories and circumstances.

Ultimately, such experiences were far more illuminating than those from a conventional travel itinerary, and taught me to navigate uncertainty and embrace discomfort. For this week’s newsletter, I spoke to three women about the journeys in India that stayed with them and what travelling here has meant to them.

One of the most memorable journeys for travel writer Shivya Nath was a trek through the Tirthan Valley in Himachal Pradesh, with a crew of local women.

Trekking has always been a male-dominated industry and Nath, author of the book Rootless and Restless, was struck by the personal journeys of the women leading the expedition. (She travelled with an organisation which trains women to become guides, overcoming social and family restrictions.) “For some of the women, spending even two nights away from home required negotiating with their families,” she told me.

The route wound through dense forests in the Great Himalayan national park, across ancient bridges, over gushing, undammed rivers, accompanied by the soundtrack of birdsong. “The only sounds you hear are of nature,” she recalled. Along the way, they stopped to forage lingri, a seasonal wild fern used in regional cooking, which they later prepared at a riverside camp they set up for the night.

The following morning, the group hiked to a waterfall, standing together on a large rock in the middle of the cascading water. Being with “just a group of women who love being in the outdoors, was a really powerful feeling for me”, she said.

For Afsha Sethi, a corporate leadership professional from Bengaluru who has travelled extensively in India, one of her most cherished travel experiences is an unlikely one. As part of an extracurricular project when she was in high school, students were paired with a local community through an NGO. She spent a week shadowing a farming family growing rice and areca nut in Karkolli, in northern Karnataka.

Walks through lush forests with waterfalls were a daily routine. “We would come back with leeches on our legs, and then put salt to get rid of them,” she recalled.

The trip also brought her into contact with something she had not encountered before. When her roommate told the host family about getting her period, their sleeping arrangements were shifted to the courtyard. “It turned out to be a great experience, because the weather was very nice and [we were] looking at the stars and just chatting late into the night,” she said. It reminded her that travelling through India can mean encountering different social conventions.

‘The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it’

The travel boom in India is led by domestic travellers. The country recorded almost 2.95bn domestic tourist visits in 2024, a jump of 17.5% from the previous year, according to recent government data. Overall, India ranks 31st in the World Economic Forum’s Travel and Tourism Development Index 2026, up eight spots since 2024.

Women accounted for 43% of foreign arrivals, an interesting counterpoint to India’s reputation as a challenging destination for female travellers.

But the boom raises questions about what happens to the places being discovered. “Social media has definitely accelerated the over-tourism problem,” says Nath. “I think one of the most basic things travellers can do is to avoid geotagging locations.” She suggests choosing community-owned tourism initiatives that allow local people to benefit directly from visitors.

Nayanika Gaffino, a mother and recreational athlete who has also clocked up the miles around the country, has seen this happen first-hand in Goa, where she lives. “The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it … even though it doesn’t have the capacity to handle so many people,” she says, adding there are beaches her family now avoids because of this.

Travelling with girls, Gaffino also has to consider practicalities such as hygiene and access to clean public toilets and safety. As such, she gravitates towards boutique properties that are accustomed to hosting families with children.

Nath acknowledges that solo travel can require extra vigilance. On solo trips, she prefers family-run homestays or guesthouses where someone is aware that she is on her own and can watch out for her. At the same time, she adds, India can be an intensely hospitable place where people generously invite you for tea or a meal.

Discovering a place, she says, should also include finding ways to preserve the very qualities that led you to it in the first place. The journeys the women described involved seeing a side of India that differed from their own expectations. This is what makes al-Qazwini’s understanding of wonder relevant for me. To approach something with wonder is not to be impressed by just beauty or uniqueness but to have a curiosity about what it might show us – its possibilities and its limitations.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Politics | More than one-third of the international delegates – about 115 people – expected to attend the Congress party’s Bharat summit over the weekend were denied entry to India or had their visas cancelled, according to the Wire. Congress described this as an attempt to stifle voices of criticism, while the foreign ministry said that visa-related decisions are “sovereign functions” but did not share further details.

Lawsuit | The US state department has served summons in a civil lawsuit brought by Khalistani separatist leader Gurpatwant Singh Pannu against national security adviser Ajit Doval and former research and analysis wing, Samant Goel, according to the Hindu. The foreign ministry described the lawsuit as “frivolous”.

Culture | A 50-year-old Navaratri festival at Wesleyan University, in Connecticut, has made it an unlikely home for Carnatic music and dance traditions. Although such festivals take place at cities around the world, few institutions have become as significant a platform for Indian music and dance.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Shaunak Sen, director of the Academy Award-nominated All That Breathes, recommends: “If you find yourself in the oft-trodden window of wanting to watch something for an hour after dinner, but feel drowned in the mediocre flotsam of the big streamers, I have a suggestion watch the strangely inventive short films of the artist-bureaucrat SNS Sastry from the 1960s and 70s [on YouTube]. Unlike the regular pedantic state films of the time, Sastry somehow created avant garde, surreal, even funny films, funded by the Films Division of India. I Am 20, which interviewed young adults born in the year of India’s independence, is an inquiry into a certain national zeitgeist. Between the candour of the participants and the sharpness of their complaints, one can scarcely believe this was made under the aegis of the government.”

Firecrackers were responsible for most of Delhi’s fine particulate matter of PM 2.5 – about 71% – in the hours after Diwali celebrations, according to a first-of-its-kind assessment by the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology. The national capital reported its most-polluted Diwali since 2021, data from the Central Pollution Control Board showed.

AI anxiety is everywhere. Pranav Bhasin’s comedy We Were Here has brought that to screens in a short film that is making an impression internationally. Bhasin’s mockumentary is about three retired men in a small Indian town who, persuaded that artificial intelligence is taking over human jobs, fight back by impersonating household appliances. One becomes a dish antenna, another a vehicle’s reverse camera and a third a microwave. The situation becomes more complicated when one of their children takes a job at an AI company. “Do you want artificial intelligence?” “No, I have real one,” goes one caustic exchange.

The New Yorker released the film on YouTube and it’s eligible for Oscars consideration after winning at the Palm Springs International ShortFest.

#136. Can Anthropic's record breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Can Anthropic's record breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

It was during November 2020 that somewhere in San Francisco, a woman called Daniela Amodei phoned a friend, asking for advice.

Daniela oversaw safety and policy at OpenAI, one of the most talked-about AI companies in the world.

But Daniela and her brother Dario - who worked there too - had misgivings over safety levels at OpenAI and thought it could be time to walk away.

So she did what many do when we can't make a decision about something, she phoned a friend. The friend's reply was direct and to the point: "You don't need to be on the phone with me. You already know the answer."

The Amodei siblings and a handful of other colleagues quit. Together, they set up a rival â Anthropic.

Six years on and Anthropic, the maker of leading AI model Claude, has predicted revenues of $52bn in 2026 and $135bn in 2027, according to analyst estimates.

It is about to sell shares to the public for the first time in what's known as an Initial Public Offering or IPO, aiming for a valuation of an estimated $1.4tn (Â£1tn), which would propel it straight into the top 10 of the most valuable US companies.

The sale comes at a crucial time when many are wondering whether, amid increasing concern about the potential of AI, the industry's bubble is showing signs of bursting.

Where once investors were "all in" on a technological revolution to rival the railroads, the car, electricity and the internet, some are now hedging their bets, amid arguments that AI prices will be driven down as the technology becomes more common and concerns that a hefty wedge of the AI investment is tech giants investing in each other.

Shares in companies that supply the raw computing power behind AI, like Coreweave and Nebius, have seen their value cut in half while even the mighty Nvidia has lost its crown as the world's most valuable company with its shares down 30% - a staggering $2tn fall in value.

So could the Anthropic IPO reinflate the bubble or will it be remembered as the last gasp of a boom that is heading for a bust?

At an event last week, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told JPMorgan Chase's CEO Jamie Dimon that his guiding principle was to "never let your company be worth a single dollar more than it's worth".

The company is selling roughly $65bn worth of shares to the general public and a further $26bn on to other big tech companies. It is comfortably the single biggest money raising event in US corporate history.

It's an old adage that during a gold rush you should buy companies that sell the picks and shovels as opposed to those staking everything on striking gold. In AI's case that's the chip makers, the data centre builders, the energy providers. But Anthropic is arguably the gold itself and this IPO will see what value people put on it, according to Fiona Cincotta.

"I think the reason that this IPO is so important is basically it's the first ability for the public investors to access a pure play AI trade," she says, adding that previous offerings have been about "other parts of the ecosystem, which are, the semiconductors, the chips, the, data centres. But this is the first time that we were actually able to access the AI provider."

Daniela Amodei helped set up a rival company to OpenAI

What a company is worth depends almost entirely on predicted future earnings.

It's that calculation that many think presents the sharpest needle to the AI bubble.

Will current and future users of AI be prepared to pay enough to justify the trillions being spent on the chips, data centres, energy and employees, some of whom can command annual salaries of over $100m?

"AI is certainly in a financial bubble. While LLMs [large language models] offer genuine utility and are undoubtedly here to stay, the economics don't add up," said Gary Marcus, Professor Emeritus at New York University and author of Taming Silicon Valley, speaking earlier this year.

"[The] more companies build AI models, the [more the] technology becomes commoditised, eliminating meaningful competitive advantages and driving down prices."

So far, the growth rates in Anthropic revenue are extraordinary.

But these explosive growth rates hide a potential problem. Around a quarter of that revenue came from just a handful of companies, among them Google and Amazon.

It's also concentrated in the US which accounts for two-thirds of its revenue.

There is also the staggering network of circular financing found in the AI world. For example, Anthropic has agreements to buy or lease hundreds of billions of dollars worth of chips and computing power from a handful of companies including Broadcom, Google, Amazon. Many of those firms are also investors in Anthropic and major customers of its products.

It's not just some Wall Street investors questioning whether the AI boom is all it's cracked up to be. Out on Main Street, ordinary Americans have concerns too.

With the US driving so much of global AI growth, a growing public backlash against a technology that threatens to replace millions of jobs and hoover up vast amounts of energy and water became a factor in the recent midterm elections in the US.

Many areas in the US have passed moratoriums on data centre development and many candidates deemed too close to big tech found that association a political liability rather than an asset. Many Democrat candidates publicly opposed data centre development and won.

Added to that, the Pied Piper of AI, chip maker Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, resigned last week after admitting to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

Dario Amodei is one of Silicon Valley's big beasts

Huang's spectacular fall from grace and Nvidia's 30% fall in value from its peak have left AI investors looking for a new champion for the AI boom that has underpinned the US stock market and arguably much of its entire economy for the last two years.

If Anthropic â and its CEO are to be AI's new Pied Piper, he will be playing a different tune to the relentlessly upbeat, leather jacket-clad talisman Huang.

While Huang wanted AI development to continue full throttle, Anthropic was built on misgivings over the levels of safety and responsibility the OpenAI leavers felt were being exercised over the development of society-changing technology.

Amodei has warned that AI capabilities could enable biological weapon development, mass surveillance, authoritarianism and the replacement of millions of white-collar, tax-paying workers.

He has called for a slowdown in its development as its powers are growing at such an exponential rate that it could very soon elude any human oversight.

That said, his paternalistic overtures have been sceptically received by those who point out that the pace of Anthropic's development of new more powerful models has shown no sign of the slowdown he's advocating for the rest of the industry.

But for all the concerns, just as with Elon Musk's SpaceX before it â the hype and the FOMO (fear of missing out) on an undoubtedly profound moment in technological and perhaps human development â means that buying interest is high.

It's reported that orders to buy shares exceed the number on offer by over six times.

With demand outstripping supply by such a margin, a first-day jump is likely given the oversubscription, but it doesn't tell you much about long-run return.

Using SpaceX as an example again, shares initially rose nearly 70% from its IPO sale price of $135 to $225. Shares in SpaceX are currently back to $145.

The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful and blow a bit of fresh new air into what some maintain still looks a lot like a bubble.

Eileen Burbidge, founder of tech investors Passion Capital, is one of those who reject the notion that AI is a bubble about to burst.

"Maybe the growth of the bubble has slowed down a little bit, but actually I wouldn't be willing to say that it's about to burst yet," says Burbidge. "I think this IPO could actually mean everyone could be off to the races again."

Fiona Cincotta believes Anthropic's IPO is about far more than just Anthropic. "I think what's going to be really interesting here is I think it can actually be used as a valuation benchmark for the entire AI ecosystem," she says.

"That's why I don't think this is just going to affect the share price of Anthropic. I think this is going to have much wider implications across the whole AI trade."

Two things can be true at the same time. The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful - FOMO is a powerful emotion in financial markets. But its probable success won't soothe the unease in some quarters that not every company, every investor and every citizen will think of AI as the answer to humanity's problems.

#135. Israel launches new ground operation in Gaza

Israel has said its military is targeting Hamas infrastructure in central Gaza and urged civilians to avoid the area. The fight is unfolding against the backdrop of a dire food and shelter situation for Palestinians.

Israel says its military is involved in operations on Wednesday in central Gaza in an effort to dislodge Hamas militants.

France's AFP news agency reported "intense Israeli artillery shelling amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes."

A statement from the Israeli military (IDF) said, "The IDF launched a precise operation within Gaza earlier today west of the Yellow Line to remove a threat posed to IDF troops operating in the Gaza Strip."

The Yellow Line in Gaza was established in an October 2025 ceasefire between Israel and Hamas, and separates Israeli and Hamas-controlled territory.

On Wednesday, COGAT, an Israeli military body responsible for civilian matters in the Palestinian territories warned international aid agencies and Palestinian civilians to avoid areas near the Al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps and Gaza's main thoroughfare, the Salah al-Din road.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Palestinian medics told Reuters news agency that one person had been killed in an Israeli airstrike in central Gaza and that two more had been killed in separate incidents unfolding in Deir al-Balah and Gaza City.

Hamas — which is listed as a terrorist organization by Israel, the US, Germany, the EU and others — blasted the Israeli operation, calling it "a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement and confirmation that the occupation has reneged on its obligations."

Israel's military countered that it was reacting to Hamas ceasefire violations and the group's attempts to rearm.

The October 2025 ceasefire halted major operations, but both sides have continued to carry out limited attacks. Gaza health authorities report at least 1,600 Palestinian deaths and Israel reports the loss of fewer than 10 soldiers since the ceasefire went into effect.

In all, Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed inside Gaza during Israel's retaliation for Hamas' October 7, 2023, assault — which killed roughly 1,200 Israelis, most of whom were civilians.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Beyond the threat of violent gunfire and airstrikes, civilians in Gaza continue to face ongoing food insecurity and lack of adequate shelter as winter approaches.

On Tuesday, the International Organization for Migration (IOM) and the UN's World Food Programme (WFP) warned that funding shortfalls and access restrictions put untold numbers of Palestinian lives at risk.

Currently, more than 1.7 million Gazans are displaced, with IOM and WFP saying 1.4 million face acute hunger and 1.98 million require shelter assistance.

With Gaza's infrastructure decimated by years of Hamas mismanagement and pounding Israeli attacks, more than 80% of Palestinians are unemployed, meaning they cannot afford food and have been forced to burn garbage to try to keep warm.

"Families in Gaza are facing their first sleepless nights of a very long winter ahead," said WFP Palestine Country Director Shaun Hughes. "Unless we get immediate support to scale up the humanitarian response, we risk this becoming not only a shelter crisis but also a deeper food security crisis."

#132. Palestinian woman shot dead by Israeli settlers near Jericho, witnesses say

Bullet hit 72-year-old Fadha Wadha in head outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta, in the West Bank, say residents

A 72-year-old Palestinian woman has been shot dead in an overnight attack by Israeli settlers in a village near Jericho where settlers have recently moved in after taking over several Palestinian homes.

Fadha Wadha was standing outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta in the occupied West Bank when she was struck in the head by a bullet as Israeli settlers fired towards Palestinian houses, according to testimonies from residents. Several other Palestinians, including her son, were wounded by gunfire.

Abu Younis, a cousin of Wadha’s husband, told the Guardian that the violence had begun when a group of settlers attacked a young boy they accused of entering a courtyard seized from its Palestinian residents.

Dozens of Israeli settlers, some of them armed, moved into eight buildings in al-Duyuk al-Tahta two weeks ago with the backing of the settler organisation Nachala and a regional council representing illegal settlements in the central West Bank.

“A vehicle carrying settlers chased him,” Younis said. “There were about 15 settlers in several vehicles. They began firing indiscriminately, killing the woman.”

Witnesses said Palestinian residents began to throw stones to defend themselves, as more settlers arrived at the scene, shooting at civilians and injuring six men.

Wadha’s son, Arafat Fidhat, was wounded after he rushed to the scene after learning that his mother had been shot. His wife, Naama Fidhat, told Reuters he was in shock when he accidentally collided with a parked car belonging to one of the settlers.

Witnesses said the vehicle was empty at the time, but that settlers responded by opening fire, wounding Arafat in the torso.

Naama Fidhat said her husband was being taken to a hospital in Ramallah when Israeli soldiers stopped the ambulance and arrested him. She said Israeli soldiers also arrested their son, Wadha’s grandson, early the following morning.

“What did we do? We didn’t do anything,” she said. “This is our house, they were the ones who came with weapons … we were unarmed, we were sleeping.”

The Israeli military said a group of Palestinians in ​a car “accelerated their vehicle toward an Israeli vehicle and rammed it”. It said the Palestinians exited the vehicle and attacked the Israelis, who opened fire at the Palestinians. “There are Israeli and Palestinian ​casualties in the incident,” the military said.

The arrival of the Israeli settlers in al-Duyuk al-Tahta had raised fears among Palestinian residents who have faced repeated attacks in the area.

The Binyamin regional council, which covers 47 illegal settlements, claimed the properties in the village had been bought from Palestinians, but Jericho’s governor, Hussein Hamayel, disputed the claim, saying the land belonged to the state and could not legally be sold.

Human rights activists said settlers had assaulted and robbed two Israeli activists and shot a Palestinian man in the leg during the takeover earlier this month.

“Since the settlers moved into the area on 4 November our lives have been turned upside down,” Younis told the Guardian. “We used to live peacefully and safely on our farms, but since the settlers arrived, our lives have become a living hell. I own 53 dunams [5.3 hectares] of farmland with greenhouses, but we are no longer allowed to access them. We have suffered devastating financial losses, and now people are being killed as well.”

There has been a sharp ​rise in attacks by Israeli settlers on Palestinians in the West Bank, territory Israel captured in the 1967 six-day war. UN bodies and most ​countries deem the settlements illegal, which Israel disputes.

On Tuesday, Suleiman Abu Srour, a photographer for the Palestinian news agency Wafa, was beaten by Israeli soldiers while covering events in al-Duyuk al-Tahta. Wafa said he suffered fractures and bruises, was detained for six hours and had his phone confiscated before being released.

Since the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attacks on Israel, there has been a surge of Israeli settlement expansion, ethnic cleansing and violence against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank. More than 1,100 Palestinians have been killed there by Israeli forces or settlers.

#131. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#130. Fiona the hippo undergoing care after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

Watch: Fiona the hippo under observation after eating hat

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona is undergoing emergency medical care after swallowing a knit hat that slipped into her enclosure.

The Zoo said that Fiona had swallowed the hat about nine days ago and was asymptomatic at first, adding that she was "large enough that a small-ish hat should be passing through".

But a few days later, her appetite started to decrease, and the zoo staff were trying to encourage Fiona to eat to help push the hat through her digestive system.

Fiona rose to fame when she was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo and at first struggled to survive. Her story inspired an ice cream flavour as well as a character in Cincinnati's ballet.

Michelle Curley, a spokesperson for the zoo, told US media that the hat got into Fiona's enclosure by accident, after a visitor tossed it in the air and it caught on a ceiling fan that flung it through a small opening.

"We probably couldn't make [it] happen again if we tried," Curley said.

Zoo staff have been providing a series of supportive measures like fluids and ultrasounds to help Fiona recover.

The latest ultrasound showed the hat passing through Fiona's small intestine, the staff said, adding that she is still a bit dehydrated.

"Surgery isn't an option for full-grown hippos and our best chance for success with Fiona would be if she could pass this on her own," one of the zookeepers said in an update.

Fiona was born six weeks early to 17-year-old hippo Bibi on 24 January 2017.

At birth she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species.

The zoo said staff were "rooting for her and hoping that she proves to be as strong and resilient as she was at the beginning of her life".

#128. Israeli parliament elects new speaker backed by Netanyahu’s opponents

Knesset’s appointment of Matan Kahana marks step towards reversing policies of outgoing government

The Israeli parliament elected a new speaker on Tuesday, a key step toward rolling back policies passed by the outgoing government led by the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu.

The new speaker is Matan Kahana, an ally of Gadi Eisenkot, the party leader currently working to form the next government and replace Netanyahu.

Eisenkot’s bloc has pledged to undo some of Netanyahu’s most contentious policies following Kahana’s election.

He was approved as speaker with 66 votes in the 120-member chamber, backed by the Jewish-led opposition parties and by the Arab-led parties. The candidate backed by Netanyahu’s bloc, Ofir Katz, received 52 votes following a stormy debate that stretched over seven hours.

“The appointment of Matan Kahana as Knesset speaker is a worthy democratic move that reflects the absolute majority in favour of change,” Eisenkot said ahead of the vote.

The vote took place weeks after the parliamentary elections on 27 October in which Netanyahu and his allies lost their parliamentary majority. Eisenkot was subsequently tasked by Israel’s president with trying to form a new coalition government.

Eisenkot has up to five more weeks to secure enough alliances to form a coalition.

As negotiations continue on the sidelines, his bloc, along with unaffiliated Arab-led parties, can use their majority in the newly sworn-in Knesset to advance their legislative agenda.

They introduced several bills last week, including to set term limits on the role of prime minister, to form a government-led inquiry into the Hamas-led 7 October 2023 attacks and to reverse laws that limit the powers of the judiciary.

Kahana gives the opposition bloc a major legislative advantage, according to Assaf Shapira of the Israel Democracy Institute, a Jerusalem thinktank.

“It’s important because there is not much time, because the speaker of the Knesset can make the legislative process much, much faster or much slower,” Shapira said.

Kahana replaced Amir Ohana, a Netanyahu ally, as speaker.

Eisenkot and his allies are under pressure to swiftly advance their legislative priorities in the weeks ahead. Roy Folkman, a member of Eisenkot’s Yashar party, heads the temporary committee that decides much of the Knesset’s work until a government is formed.

If Eisenkot fails to form a government, the Israeli president could give Netanyahu an opportunity to do so. That would probably give Netanyahu’s Likud party control of the committee.

Even though Netanyahu’s opponents would still hold a majority, they would “feel that their momentum has gone”, Shapira said, and there would be a “real danger that some of the legislation will be delayed”.

Chamber procedures will pose a further challenge. A bill has to be introduced on the floor, considered in committee and voted on several times on the floor before it passes the Knesset. That winding process could make it hard to move bills across the finish line before time runs out.

Shapira said Eisenkot and his allies are likely to prioritise rolling back controversial laws that gave the government a larger role in appointing judges and weakened the power of Israel’s attorney general, an independent role that is seen by many as central to Israel’s system of government checks and balances.

“It’s very easy to abolish these laws because no new institution has to be created,” he said.

Establishing a body to investigate the 7 October attacks would be easier, Shapira added, and carry greater legitimacy once a government is formed.

Netanyahu’s opponents argue he has blocked an independent probe into any military or intelligence failures around the day, the deadliest in Israel’s history, and evaded accountability.

#125. Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years in US prison over Trump murder plot

Asif Merchant, allegedly linked to Iran, found by court to have sought retribution over Qassem Suleimani death

A Pakistani man was sentenced to more than 27 years in prison on Tuesday for plotting to assassinate Donald Trump in retaliation for the US killing of Revolutionary Guards commander Qassem Suleimani, the Department of Justice said.

Asif Merchant, who had alleged ties to Iran, sought to hire a hitman to assassinate a politician or a government official in the United States, a Brooklyn court found.

Suleimani, the head of Iran’s foreign military operations, was killed in a US drone strike in Baghdad in January 2020. Iranian officials have repeatedly vowed to avenge his killing.

Merchant had testified that he was forced into the plot to protect his family in the Iranian capital Tehran from the Guards, adding that he thought he would get caught before anyone was killed, multiple media outlets reported.

He said he was never ordered to kill a specific person but noted his Iranian contact had mentioned three people in connection with the plot – Trump, the former US president Joe Biden and former UN ambassador Nikki Haley.

But Todd Blanche, the United States attorney general, insisted on Tuesday that the plot was against Trump.

“An Iranian agent who came to the United States to coordinate an assassination attempt against President Trump has been justly sentenced for his criminal conduct,” Blanche said after Merchant was sentenced to 330 months’ imprisonment.

“The Department of Justice will not tolerate foreign terrorist plots against our leaders and will not stop until those responsible are held accountable.”

US officials previously said Merchant had “close ties to Iran” and described his alleged plot as “straight out of the Iranian regime’s playbook”.

Merchant was arrested on 12 July 2024 as he planned to leave the country.

#124. Would Ukraine trade land for NATO membership or protection?

The US is exploring whether Ukraine would cede parts of Donetsk province still under its control in return for NATO membership or protection. Experts warn the idea is risky.

The US has asked Ukraine whether it would be willing to cede Donetsk province to Russia in return for NATO membership. Together, Donetsk and Luhansk constitute the so-called Donbas region. Russia currently controls most of Luhansk province, and large swathes of Donetsk, though Ukraine continues holding a number of major cities there, including Kramatorsk and Sloviansk.

So far, no concrete proposal had been put forward, according to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy. He said he would publicly discuss the matter once the US and NATO member states had agreed on the plan, stressing that he could not make any such decision "on his own." Zelenskyy also said any potential agreement would go ahead "without recognizing Russia's occupation of these territories."

The Ukrainian leader said the US was talking about guarantees similar to those enshrined in Articles 4 and 5 of the North Atlantic Treaty, though did not envision US troops on Ukrainian soil. Article 4 provides for consultations among member states in the event of imminent threats, while Article 5 stipulates that an attack on one state is considered an attack on all. The US, however, has said it pictures bilateral guarantees for Ukraine similar to those established with Israel and certain Middle Eastern countries.

Russia has yet to comment on these proposals, with President Putin's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov stating that "we maintain our position that, in the interest of the peace process, it is not appropriate to disclose details of the talks between us and the Americans."

Experts interviewed by DW are skeptical of the land-for-security-guarantees idea, objecting that the US cannot uphold such an arrangement on its own.

"It seems to me that this is what happens to normal diplomacy when it's been run by real estate agents who are used to selling people something that doesn't really exist," Oleksandr Kraiev of the North America-Israel Research Program at Ukraine's Ukrainian Prism think tank tells DW. He says that at least half of NATO's member states have reservations about Ukraine's accession.

"We'll just end up with a second Budapest Memorandum, it'll just carry a different name," Kraiev told DW. The original 1994 Budapest Memorandum stipulated that Russia, the US and UK respect Ukraine's independence and sovereignty in its existing borders and refrain from the threat or use of force to change its territorial integrity. Ukraine in return committed to giving up all nuclear weapons.

The Budapest Memorandum, however, did not contain any commitment by the UK or US to defend Ukraine in the event of an attack.

Most experts interviewed by DW are critical of the idea of holding a referendum to let ordinary Ukrainians decide whether their country should hand over full control of Donetsk province in exchange for NATO membership.

This is because ceding territory would not only violate Ukraine's constitution and harm its national interests, but would also undermine a world order "in which borders cannot be redrawn by force," Oleksandr Khara of Ukraine's Centre for Defence Strategies told DW. "It doesn't matter whether the citizens of Ukraine vote for or against such a proposal," Khara said, adding that any such referendum would set "a very dangerous precedent."

A survey by the Kyiv International Institute of Sociology (KISS) conducted between October 8 and 16 found that 61% of respondents categorically reject withdrawing troops from Ukrainian-controlled parts of the Donbas, such as cities like Kramatorsk and Sloviansk, even in exchange for US and European security guarantees. 35% of respondents said they would begrudgingly accept a land-for-security deal.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Defense analyst Khara doubts a bilateral agreement between Ukraine and the US, akin to those established between the US and Israel, would guarantee Kyiv any more support than it presently receives.

"This concerns financial support, joint weapons production and development, and the training of Ukrainian troops — everything that falls below the threshold of collective security," Khara told DW. "It is good that these commitments — security agreements, political declarations, and the allocation of funds in the budgets of the countries supporting us — are set out in writing, at least for the next five years." Yet to prevent another war, Khara believes that ideally, Ukraine needs "a nuclear shield," specifically, a "strategic one from the US" and a "tactical one from Europe."

Ukrainian experts already floated to the idea of an alternative security arrangement to NATO members in December 2023, says Sergiy Solodkyy of Ukraine's New Europe research center. The arrangement would see Ukraine join the alliance within its internationally recognized borders, though specific protocols would stipulate that only territories under Kyiv's control would be defended.

This arrangement could be modeled on the German case, where West Germany became a member of NATO first, while East Germany joined the alliance only after German reunification.

West Germany never gave up on reunification, Solodkyy told DW. "That means we, too, should not give up on our territory or recognize the border." That is why he says the land-for-security deal proposed to Ukraine is unacceptable.

"This is a trap that Russia and the US negotiators are trying to lure the Ukrainian side into," Solodkyy told DW.

#122. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#119. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

The EI settlement expansion, due to have been announced on Thursday, has long been seen as qualitatively different since the land – between Jerusalem and Ma’aleh Adumim, a West Bank settlement city of nearly 40,000 residents – bisects the West Bank and cuts it off from East Jerusalem.

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

Bennett said: “The British Labour government’s hostility, bias and one-sided approach are particularly glaring given its failure to take any action against the Palestinian Authority, which continues to encourage terrorism through financial payments, promote incitement in its education system and engage in illegal construction in violation of signed agreements.”

Although the Israeli election was largely fought on domestic security-related issues, Eisenkot seems to be more sensitive to the implications of Israel’s crumbling global reputation, and the need to make efforts to be seen to be listening to the criticism.

If he becomes prime minister, Eisenkot will, indirectly at least, also have to take account of the views of the Arab parties even if these parties are not going to become members of the government.

The group Peace Now welcomed the attorney general’s decision: “A government that lost the elections is not permitted to exploit its final days in power to advance destructive moves that will endanger Israel’s future and its international standing.

Construction in E1 is liable to eliminate the possibility of a two-state solution and drag Israel into a severe diplomatic crisis and international sanctions. The AG’s decision is an important step, but the struggle is not over yet.”

#118. American soldier arrested by UK police on suspicion of spying taken into US military custody

Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested on 10 November

A US Army officer arrested in the UK on suspicion of spying for Russia earlier this month has been taken into US military custody, British counter-terror police have said.

The serviceman has been identified as Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner, BBC News understands. The 41-year-old - who served in the US Army since 2008 - was arrested near Gloucester last week by British counter-terror police.

Tyner was based at Imjin Barracks in Innsworth, Gloucestershire, the headquarters of Nato's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps.

Counter Terrorism Policing said he is now in US custody over suspected national security and espionage allegations. At the time of his arrest, Russia said it had "nothing to do with it".

British police previously said he was being held in London after he was arrested under section one and section three of the National Security Act 2023, which relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The day after Tyner's arrest on 10 November, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said he was "unaware" of what it was connected to.

"We have nothing to do with it, and it has nothing to do with us," he told reporters in Moscow.

The US serviceman is understood to have been based at Imjin Barracks in Gloucestershire

On Tuesday, Assistant Commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of Counter Terrorism Policing, said: "Subsequently, a decision was made to pass the investigation to them [their American partners] to progress."

"These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place," he added.

Tyner's arrest was the result of a long-running operation by MI5 - the UK's domestic intelligence and security agency - in collaboration with the FBI, the BBC understands.

He had previously served in Afghanistan and Iraq, but at the time of his arrest was working with NATO's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps near Gloucester - where he was said to be entrusted with sensitive information which would have been extremely damaging if leaked to Russia.

Detectives sent the Crown Prosecution Service a file on the senior US soldier's alleged spying for consideration - allegations described by the head of Counter Terrorism Policing as "extremely serious."

But at that stage the Attorney General's Office in London got involved. As did the US authorities.

And now the decision has been taken that Lieutenant Colonel Tyner should be taken into custody by the US Military.

It means he will not stand trial in the UK, but under US Uniform Code of Military Justice.

FBI director Kash Patel said an army officer was in custody "as the result of an espionage investigation and the close cooperation" between the bureau, US Army and various UK partners.

"Together we worked to identify a potential threat to national security and take swift action," he said.

"This FBI has zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk."

US Brig Gen Sean Stinchon said the case should serve as a "stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath" they all take.

"Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardising our collective defence, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served," he added.

#117. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The military ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

#114. US loses measles elimination status after 26 years amid record case numbers

BBC's Madeline Halpert explains why the US lost its measles elimination status and what it could mean.

After more than two decades, the US has lost its measles-free status, following two years of record case numbers and deaths.

The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a specialised service of the United Nations, determined the US has had uninterrupted transmission of the same strain of measles for more than 12 months.

Dr Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, announced that Mexico and Bolivia have also lost their measles elimination status, attributing the resurgence in the region to persistent "gaps in vaccination".

The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) said it was committed to "regaining measles elimination status" by expanding access to vaccines.

The US has seen 5,400 cases of measles this year alone, with 10 measles-related deaths, the most the country has seen since the 1990s.

The measles virus - with its signature blotchy red rash - used to infect millions of children a year before a vaccine was invented. The extremely contagious virus can be fatal, and some patients can experience long-term complications including encephalitis and immune amnesia, which resets the immune system, leaving it with only limited ability to respond to new infections.

The US recommends two measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) shots for children between ages one and six, which is 97% effective in protecting against the illness.

PAHO, a group within the World Health Organization, revoked the US's measles-elimination after determining that the virus had circulated nonstop for more than a year.

"These figures are a serious warning for our region," PAHO's director Barbosa said at a news conference on Tuesday. "Measles is resurging globally amid persistent gaps in vaccination."

He added that Canada, which lost its status of having eliminated measles last year after seeing several large outbreaks, has made strides this year to interrupt transmission.

Daniel Salas, the executive manager of immunisations at PAHO, said that the US and others who lost elimination status would be required to submit corrective action plans outlining how they would attempt to improve vaccination rates.

US Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr Kennedy has attempted to make a series of sweeping changes to US vaccine recommendations and policies. The vaccine sceptic has also frequently cast doubt on the safety of MMR shot, as vaccination rates have declined across the US in recent years.

Kennedy has argued that the rise in measles cases started before he took the helm at HHS and that neighbouring countries have also seen more infections.

Responding to Tuesday's announcement, the CDC said the agency and its partners were "committed to stopping measles outbreaks, protecting Americans, and regaining measles elimination status".

"Together, we will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to vaccines, and help communities close gaps in protection," the agency said.

CDC Director Erica Schwartz said the measles elimination designation was not what protected Americans.

"Vaccination does," she said. "We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities."

In a post on social media, Kennedy said: "I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles."

"We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," he also said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment."

The UK, Spain and Austria were among European nations that lost their measles-free status earlier this year, along with countries including Russia, Azerbaijan and Uzbekistan.

Nuwan Gunawardhana, an assistant professor of medicine with Columbia University Irving Medical Center, said the US losing its elimination status was "a huge leap backwards".

The US was the first country to eliminate measles in the Americas thanks to vaccination campaigns, noted William Moss, a paediatric infectious disease specialist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health.

Dr Gunawardhana said it took massive efforts from public health officials and clinicians to build trust with patients to increase uptake of the MMR vaccine, leading to decades in the US with few infections and deaths.

But that trust has been steadily eroding since the Covid-19 pandemic, when Dr Gunawardhana said patients were confronted with misinformation about the safety of vaccines and lost even more trust in public health systems.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity, which limits the spread of measles and protects those who cannot get vaccinated, including newborns.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity - which limits the spread of measles

An outbreak in Pennsylvania has infected over 1,400 people - likely a large underestimate - and killed seven people, according to state officials.

Kennedy, the health secretary, has sparred with Pennsylvania officials over how it is counting the measles deaths, with CDC declining for now to record some of the deaths in its database.

Last year, the health secretary fired all the members of a top vaccine advisory panel and replaced them with his own appointees, some of whom were vaccine critics.

The panel recommended a number of vaccine changes - including no longer requiring the Hepatitis B vaccine for all newborns - but those recommendations were blocked by a federal judge.

President Donald Trump this year also cast doubt on the safety of the MMR vaccine, issuing an executive order for the shot to be split into three separate immunisations for measles, mumps and rubella, a move public health experts say is costly and could sow confusion about an already safe vaccine.

Doctors and public health officials say the misinformation from Kennedy and Trump has played a major role in falling vaccination rates.

"It's confusing patients. It's confusing families," Dr Gunawardhana said. "We need a clear message from the top."

The federal government's vaccine manoeuvres have left public health officials and states to fill in the gaps, offering their own separate recommendations upholding decades-long practices.
