#113. Alexander Zverev notified of abuse lawsuit by ex-partner during ATP Finals

Men’s world No 1 allegedly violated terms of settlement

Lawyers say Zverev will ‘defend himself against lawsuit’

Alexander Zverev was officially notified of a lawsuit filed by his former partner during the middle of the season-ending ATP Finals on Wednesday. The latest legal case against the men’s world No 1 brought by the mother of the former couple’s daughter alleges that Zverev violated the terms of an out-of-court settlement between the pair after the woman accused him of assaulting her.

The lawsuit brought by Brenda Patea in Germany takes issue with Zverev reportedly saying in an interview with the French sports daily L’Equipe earlier this year that “it has been proven that the accusations were false” and “my innocence has been proven”.

Zverev’s Berlin-based lawyers, Christian Schertz and Anna Sophie Heuchemer, confirmed in a statement that they were formally served with Patea’s lawsuit on Wednesday.

“Our client will defend himself against the lawsuit,” Zverev’s lawyers said. “If the statement attributed to him was indeed made in the form alleged, we consider it to be a permissible expression of opinion” – which would be protected by the German constitution. They said there had been no breach of any contractual obligations.

Patea’s lawyer, Michael Scheele, said that Zverev’s claim over his innocence “triggered a veritable storm of criticism against our client”. He said the backlash was damaging to her business as an influencer and that she also felt her honour had been violated.

“Quite apart from the fact that Zverev’s claim is false, he was not permitted to make any public statements whatsoever, in any form,” he said. “Both Brenda Patea and Alexander Zverev had agreed to this in a settlement.”

Patea is seeking that Zverev be ordered not to repeat such comments and that he be fined up to €250,000 (£208,500) every time if he does.

Zverev faced a charge of causing bodily harm to Patea during an argument in Berlin in May 2020. He always denied any wrongdoing. The case ended in a district court in Berlin in 2024 after the settlement. The case officially declared no verdict and the settlement entailed no acknowledgment of guilt on Zverev’s part. Zverev agreed to pay fines to the state and charitable organisations.

The German player this month ascended to the world No 1 ranking for the first time. He also won his first two grand slam titles this year and is competing this week at the ATP Finals in Turin.

#112. US Pentagon chief Hegseth signs military deal in Venezuela

On a visit to Caracas, Pete Hegseth signed a deal that signifies the "reestablishment of our defense partnership." US-Venezuela ties were strained for years until US troops ousted ex-President Nicolas Maduro in January.

US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth visited Venezuela on Wednesday, met with the country's interim authorities and also signed a military cooperation deal with the Latin American country.

The Pentagon did not release any information on Hegseth's trip or confirm the visit, although it was broadcast on Venezuelan state media.

The visit comes after US forces in January ousted former Venezuelan President Nicolas Maduro and took him along with his wife to New York to face drug trafficking charges. Since then, US President Donald Trump has backed acting President Delcy Rodriguez to lead the country.

Both Rodriguez and Hegseth touted the new military agreement between the two countries at the presidential palace in Caracas.

Rodriguez said the deal would strengthen training for the Venezuelan military while facilitating access to new technologies. She said this would allow the two nations to "face common threats."

Hegseth said the US sees the deal "as a reestablishment of our defense partnership and the opportunity to work together between our two great militaries."

The agreement includes intelligence sharing between the two nations in addition to joint training.

The Pentagon chief said the US seeks "to ensure the Venezuelan people are not threatened by foreign actors or narco-terrorists or criminals and that they are safe and secure inside sovereign borders."

Hegseth said Wednesday that since Maduro's ouster, Venezuela "has gone from our most aggressive regional threat to a compliant security partner."

US-Venezuela relations were strained under Maduro, who had taken office in 2013. In 2019, Maduro cut off Venezuela's formal diplomatic relations with the US after Trump's first administration recognized Venezuelan opposition leader Juan Guaido as the country's interim president.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the ouster of Maduro, the Trump administration urged Venezuela to comply with demands such as US access to the Venezuelan oil market. Trump had threatened Rodriguez, who was Venezuela's vice president, with a "fate worse than Maduro" if she didn't cooperate with Washington.

Trump has later praised Rodriguez as a "terrific person" who is doing a "great job."

Under Rodriguez, Venezuela has released political prisoners and facilitated the shipments of oil to the US, with the US then controlling the proceeds and returning the money under an energy deal. US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has been overseeing Venezuela's transition and is in close contact with Rodriguez.

Elections after Maduro's ouster are expected at some point in Venezuela, but with Rodriguez's government acting favorably towards the US, it's unclear whether they would happen anytime soon. Nobel Prize winner and opposition leader Maria Corina Machado recently urged elections after arriving in Venezuela.

#110. Putin's Crimean Bridge partly collapses as Zelensky hails 'unprecedented' attack by Ukraine

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimea bridge

Ukraine has severely damaged a critical bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea, in what President Volodymyr Zelensky says was an "unprecedented operation" weeks in the making.

Images shared by Ukrainian officials show a section of the Crimean Bridge collapsed into the water, dealing a logistical and symbolic blow to Moscow's war effort.

The Kremlin said it would rebuild the bridge "better than before".

Meanwhile in Kyiv, Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko said traffic had partially resumed on the Pivnichnyi (Northern) and Pivdennyi (Southern) bridges - key crossings over the Dnipro River - after they had been closed following Russian strikes overnight.

Ukraine has long targeted the Crimean Bridge, which provides a road and rail route for Russian soldiers heading towards the front line, but has struggled to close it for good.

While Kyiv has been trying to increase the cost to Russia of waging war economically, by hitting oil refineries and depots, striking the bridge is an attempt to increase the political cost on Russian President Vladimir Putin.

The timing of the strike comes at a significant moment, ahead of Putin meeting the leaders of China and the US. This could cloud his moment back in the spotlight.

The Crimean Bridge was built by Russia after it invaded and illegally annexed Ukraine's southern peninsula in 2014.

The $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) piece of infrastructure is widely seen in Russia and Ukraine as Putin's personal pet project: His close tycoon friend Arkady Rotenberg built it, and then Putin personally opened it by driving a lorry over it in May 2018.

Putin (centre) personally opened the Crimean Bridge by driving a lorry over it in May 2018

In Ukraine, the much-hated bridge is seen as one of the main symbols of Russia's continuing occupation of Ukrainian lands.

"We're drinking champagne!" Andrii, an engineer who lives in Kyiv, told the BBC.

He said the latest attack would "deal a very heavy blow to the morale of the Russians".

Some Ukrainians are concerned that Putin might now respond by carrying out even more attacks against civilian infrastructure, but Andrii said: "I don't think the Russians can make things much worse; they're already pounding us every single night with everything they've got."

Images show the damage to the Crimean Bridge from the overnight attack

The damage after the overnight attack, involving cruise missiles and drones, appeared to be far more severe than during previous strikes on the bridge, with a large arched section apparently destroyed.

Video footage appeared to show critical damage to the road section, with remnants of the support arch and road deck seen protruding from the water below.

Zelensky said the strike was the result of a seven-stage operation by the military and security services, which saw Ukrainian forces "gradually destroy air defence systems, radars, electronic warfare equipment, and other designated targets on the way to the final objective".

He said several types of Ukrainian drones as well as the country's Flamingo and Neptune cruise missiles were fired overnight during a "pivotal stage".

The Ukrainian president also said the rail section parallel to the bridge's road had also been hit by a wave of drones, though images showed its supporting arch was still standing and Russia's transport ministry later said some rail services resumed.

Ukraine's state security service SBU said its operations had "systematically suppressed enemy air defence systems" over a month, and said it showed "no Russian defence systems can guarantee the safety of illegal objects occupied by the invaders".

Meanwhile, Ukraine's military said the roadway on the bridge is "out of action", with no vehicle able to cross it, and the railway section is still standing, but with a damaged arch.

The Kremlin said Putin had been briefed on the overnight attack. Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said the bridge would "be restored following Ukrainian strikes, and it will be even better than before", and did not rule out the possibility the strikes were linked to Putin's visit to China.

Russia's Investigative Committee confirmed structural damage and said it had opened a "criminal case... on terrorism charges" after attacks by "Ukraine's armed formations".

For the second day in a row, Russia recorded the biggest Ukrainian drone attack since the war began, with 2,256 drones downed, according to the defence ministry.

Zelensky had warned of major attacks in the days leading up to a meeting between Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping in China's Shenzhen on Thursday.

Watch: BBC reports from Kyiv bridge hit by Russian attack

In Kyiv, loud explosions were heard just before midnight on Monday, with Klitschko saying shortly after that the city had suffered "damage to critical infrastructure systems" and disruption to heating and water supplies.

On Tuesday, Zelensky said four ballistic missiles had targeted the Southern Bridge, and another three had been fired on the Northern Bridge.

Some of Kyiv's above-ground metro services were also damaged in the overnight attacks, local officials said, after civilians were told to go to civil defence shelters.

Eight major bridges span the Dnipro River, connecting the western and eastern parts of Kyiv, and an estimated three million residents rely on the bridges to travel across the city.

Elsewhere, three people have been injured in a Russian missile attack in the central Cherkasy region, according to local officials.

The long-term impact of the Crimean Bridge strike is still not fully clear, though the risk of a serious transport and logistics crisis for the Russian military has increased significantly.

The collapse of the arched section has cut off the peninsula's direct road and rail links with Russia â at least until the crossing can be repaired.

However, there is still a land corridor running through the occupied parts of Ukraine's southern Zaporizhzhia and Kherson regions - though Kyiv has been systematically striking transport routes across northern Crimea, as well as bridges and other crossings in the area around Chongar.

This has already caused problems with the delivery of fuel and other supplies, including military cargo.

There is a ferry crossing across the Kerch Strait, which connects the Black Sea with the Sea of Azov. It could partly compensate for the loss of the bridge, but its capacity is very limited and it is also vulnerable to attack.

Russia could also supply Crimea by sea, but this route is also risky, as Ukrainian naval and aerial drones operate extensively in the area.

In addition, debris from the Crimean Bridge â particularly its enormous arch â may have blocked the shipping channel.

This would make it harder for larger vessels to pass through and create further problems for the Russian-occupied ports in the Sea of Azov, including Mariupol and Berdyansk.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022. Approximately one-fifth of Ukraine is now under Russian occupation and Kyiv has refused Putin's demand to give up areas of Ukraine's eastern Donbas that remain under its control.

#109. Cheese, pet passports and youth mobility: what’s in the UK-EU ‘relationship reset’ draft?

Leaked text of 72-paragraph second draft shows agreement has largely been reached before expected signoff on Friday

The UK-EU “relationship reset” agreement, which began in earnest under Keir Starmer, is expected to be signed off by Andy Burnham in Brussels on Friday.

A leaked text of the second draft shows an agreement has largely been reached with some outstanding points.

Here’s what is and isn’t addressed in the 72-paragraph joint communique, from pet passports and cheese imports to student fees and artist touring visas.

You may not make it. The deal states it will “allow people from the EU and the UK to reside for up to three years to engage in activities such as work, studies, au pairing, volunteering, or simply travelling”.

But the deal may not be active until 2030 because work permits are the preserve of national governments, not something Brussels can solve.

“We aim to apply this agreement no later than 2028 and endeavour to ensure that opportunities under the scheme will be available to our young people no later than January 2030,” the draft says.

This has yet to be agreed. The two options in the draft text are a halfway house between international and home fees, or a concession from the UK to allow EU citizens pay the same as British students (or Irish ones, who are also eligible for home fees under a separate longstanding agreement).

The choices, according to the draft, are “significantly reduced undergraduate” or “equal treatment” with UK nationals on tuition fees.

The British government wants to cap youth mobility at between 50,000 and 80,000 places to reduce impact on overall migration numbers. The EU is pushing for at least 100,000.

Some EU states, including Germany, are open to the idea of allowing unlimited numbers of British young people to work, live or study in their countries regardless of UK reciprocation.

Elton John has long been among those demanding the government fix a flaw in the Brexit deal which saw gigging bands or theatre groups unable to tour Europe without major bureaucracy (and its associated cost) since 2021.

The EU has resisted negotiating on the issue because, like youth mobility, it touches on work permits which are a national remit. But in what appears to be a win for the UK, touring artists do feature in the draft text.

No deal been reached, but it has been agreed that “the EU and the UK should explore further mutually beneficial solutions” for “touring artists”.

Not yet. The draft agreement says the sanitary and phyto-sanitary (SPS) agreement will not be implemented until 1 March 2028.

This takes into account primary legislation that is needed on the UK side to dynamically align with EU standards.

No. These are tied to the SPS deal, meaning British travellers will still have to fork out up to £200 every time they take their dog or cat to an EU country.

Travel and transport officials are hoping relevant changes to the SPS agreement can be brought forward.

“Around 15% of our customers have pets with them. For them it will make a huge difference,” Yann Leriche, the chief executive of Getlink, the owner of Eurotunnel, told the Guardian.

No. The EU has pressed the UK to follow its trade defence measures which include quotas on Chinese hybrid-electric cars and tariffs on fully electric cars.

The UK has said it will not be coerced into a decision but is demanding that British manufacturers are not locked out of the upcoming Made in Europe legislation in exchange for forming a kind of trade club with the EU on autos.

Both sides agree to protectionist measures in “a particular supply chain” but there is no agreement yet on how that will apply. According to the text, the EU proposes that “the UK would be bound to ensure in its policies and national legal order the same outcomes” as measures imposed by the EU. The UK’s proposed text says: “We will aim to protect supply chains consistently across our markets and guard against potential risks of circumvention.”

It’s gone. Instead of a vague “trusted partnership”, both sides commit to a “partnership for economic resilience”.

The language in the latest draft agreement has also been tightened up in a concession to the UK, say diplomats.

The UK wants to restore pre-Brexit access to EU databases but this will not be agreed on Friday. The EU will instead explore giving the UK access to its proposed Prüm International scheme “on data exchange for police cooperation to chosen third-country partners”.

There is no mention of Eurodac, the database that stores biometrics of asylum seekers in the EU, and diplomats say sharing this data, which was not collected for law enforcement purposes, could be tricky.

But according to the draft, the EU will “continue exploring ways to reinforce mutual and reciprocal exchanges of data on criminal records of third-country nationals”.

#107. The leave campaign has been resurrected, so how should Burnham respond?

No 10 is confident the PM can make the case for closer EU ties, while others worry about leaving the campaign solely in his hands

The launch of a pro-Brexit campaign was one of the few genuine surprises this week in Westminster, even if the arguments were straight out of a familiar playbook.

The video launched on X over the weekend, with scenes of riots in France, champagne flowing in Brussels, and European politicians saying how desperate they were for Britain’s cash.

Among Westminster’s anti-Brexit veterans, there was immediate chatter about whether a counteroffensive was needed. “I think the immediate reaction was – are they off to the races while we are still putting our pants on?” one said.

For now, the rebuttal of Stay Out’s claims – including that returning to the EU would cost £750m a week – is mostly being done on social media by interested pro-rejoin freelancers, without any kind of central organisation.

One MP said: “It is hard for us to begin any kind of new pro-rejoin campaign until we know exactly the direction the government is heading.

“There are organisations that exist already, like Best for Britain, but I think making the case to the public would need a fresh movement. I think we want to stay away from the kind of marches that People’s Vote movement did with the blue berets if we can possibly avoid it.”

Inside No 10, there is a confidence that Andy Burnham can make his own case for a closer relationship with Europe, without the need for any external pressure group. Stay Out – which is run by the former deputy chief Brexit negotiator Oliver Lewis – will be dubbed “the status quo campaign”.

There is reason to be confident. Strategists have seen Labour polling that shows almost three-quarters – 71% – of all voters support a closer relationship with Europe if it helps bring down prices and grow the economy.

It will be the prime minister, they say, who can front any campaigning that is needed, who can argue that Brexit has done more harm than good, damaging the economy, making it harder for British people to travel and businesses to trade and fuelling the small boats crisis.

However, there will be some fears that this has been tried before when David Cameron attempted to make the case for remain in 2016. But Labour strategists say this is a fundamentally different political moment. “Cameron could not make this kind of emotional connection with people, it was business leaders, facts and figures, and he was arguing for the status quo,” one said. “We are the change campaign now.”

While some cabinet members urged caution last week over Burnham’s uncertain direction on Europe, most trust the prime minister can set out a viable post-summit plan. “All of us, honestly, are slightly running to catch up with a different way of doing things,” one said. “We all easily lapse into the worries about leave-voting seats.

“But there is obviously an argument to be made that the old rules don’t apply. And Andy’s way of doing things is laying out political argument. And we have to believe in that as the way to affect public sentiment.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

There are some close to Labour who worry about leaving the argument solely to Burnham. “Can they do this from the bowels of Downing Street?” one asked. “You do need rebuttal out there and people ready to go out and fight and say, this is bollocks. And it felt like that infrastructure is not there yet.”

Another veteran of the remain campaign said there was concern about being “left out on a limb” if a rejoin campaign was resurrected. “I think whether they like it or not, there will be an apolitical campaign trying to push the maximalist position on Europe. They have people willing to help but it’s not organised in any way.”

Many of those who worked on the remain campaign and the subsequent People’s Vote are scarred by the experience, particular the final months which descended into bitter infighting. But the same is also true for some of the most high-profile veterans of Vote Leave – who also want to steer clear of Stay Out.

As in the pro-Brexit campaigns, there are often willing funders to push pro-European campaigning. “It will happen because there will be money for it,” one campaign veteran said. “But you could not pay me any money to go back to that issue.”

#106. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#105. What the Crimean bridge attack means for Russia

Ukraine's latest strike on the Kerch Bridge has disrupted a key transport link between Russia and Crimea. Beyond the damage, the attack could have serious consequences for Russian military logistics and Putin's prestige.

It had been a year and a half since Ukraine last attacked the 19-kilometer-long Kerch Bridge connecting Russian-annexed Crimea with Russia's Krasnodar region.

Now, Ukraine has struck again. The attack during the night of November 17 caused the highway section of the bridge to collapse into the sea. As a result, shipping traffic between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov has also been disrupted.

Russia's ministry of transport announced a "temporary suspension" of road and rail traffic on the bridge, though later, rail services were resumed. Russian authorities have launched a criminal investigation into what they are calling a "terrorist attack." It was only after this announcement that the Security Service of Ukraine (SBU) confirmed the attack.

The Kerch Strait bridge is considered one of Russian President Vladimir Putin's most prestigious projects. Its construction began after Russia's 2014 annexation of the Ukrainian Crimea peninsula, an act considered illegal under international law. The Kerch Bridge was completed four years later.

It has been targeted by Ukraine on several occasions since Russia's 2022 full-scale invasion. The most well-known strike occurred in October 2022, when an explosives-laden truck detonated on the bridge, causing extensive damage and several months of traffic restrictions. The most recent attack took place in June 2025, when the SBU reported that an underwater support structure was blown up.

The latest Ukrainian operation lasted about a month, and consisted of seven distinct phases. "First, the SBU and armed forces systematically neutralized the enemy's air defense system. After that, the direct attack on the bridge began," says the SBU Telegram channel.

Some Russian military bloggers claim that the attack was carried out with the support of Ukraine's Western allies, or at least using their equipment and weapons.

Ukraine has not confirmed this. It has instead pointed out that various Ukrainian-made weapons were used in the attack, ranging from a variety of drones, to FP-5 Flamingo and R-360 Neptun missiles. British daily The Times has cited Ukrainian military sources, saying that Ukraine attacked the bridge without the support of SpaceX's satellite network system Starlink. There has been no confirmation of this so far.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, meanwhile, described the strike as a response to Russian attacks: "Russia is attacking our infrastructure every day, particularly bridges in Zaporizhzhia, Dnipro, Kyiv, and the Odesa region." He said "we are rebuilding our infrastructure, but we also remind Russia that the war always returns to where it began."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the attack, pictures and videos of the bridge were shared on social media. The Krimskij Veter Telegram channel has highlighted damage to the railroad link and questioned why Russian authorities have opted to resume train services.

The Kerch Strait shipping channel is also completely blocked by debris. "This brings road traffic to a standstill, but also shipping between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov," the channel writes.

Indeed, the Kerch Strait navigational channel is merely 120 meters wide, writes Mychajlo Hontschar of the Ukrainian Center for Global Studies "Strategy XXI" on Facebook. "The strait is shallow, only three to five meters deep."

As early as the 19th century, the seabed was dredged to allow heavier ships to pass from the Black Sea to the Sea of Azov and vice versa. "Now, Vladimir Putin's bridge-building efforts have brought shipping to an indefinite standstill," Hontschar said.

Not only will river-sea vessels no longer be able to leave the waters of the Sea of Azov, the Don, and the Volga, but boats and small ships from the Black Sea fleet and the Caspian flotilla based in Novorossiysk will also be unable to do so.

Many pro-Kremlin Telegram channels have been quick to claim that the bridge does not play an important role in the Russian army's logistics or in supplying Crimea.

The Dva Mayora (Two Majors) channel quotes the "Deputy President of the Republic of Crimea," Olga Kurlayeva, who described the bridge as a "purely civilian structure."

Ukrainian military experts interviewed by DW, on the other hand, point to the systematic disruption of Russian logistics. These efforts also included regular attacks on the R-80 highway leading through Russian-occupied territories of the Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson regions.

Russia cannot replace the Kerch Bridge with any other land connection, says Oleksandr Kovalenko, a Ukrainian military expert with the Information Resistance Group that seeks to counter disinformation. He expects the damaged bridge will cause supply problems for the Russian armed forces: "If there is a resource shortage during attacks or in defense, then either the attacks or the defense will fail."

That is why he does not rule out a decline in attacks from occupied Crimea on the rest of Ukraine. "There are many launch pads for kamikaze drones in Crimea — in the Hvardijsk area, at Cape Chauda and in Balaklava," Kovalenko told DW. "From there, many drones are launched toward Odesa, Mykolaiv, and the right bank of the Dnipro River in the Kherson region. They also fly over Crimea en route toward Ukraine. Then there are attacks using Iskander-M ballistic missiles, as well as Zircon and Oniks missiles, guided missiles, and glide bombs. And while they don't originate in Crimea, they are brought over via the Kerch Bridge."

Kovalenko says the bridge strike has dealt a serious blow to Vladimir Putin's standing. It comes at a sensitive time as Putin is about to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and US President Donald Trump in Shenzhen, China.

Memes and witty comments about the bridge abound on X. Meanwhile, the Russian president's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has promised to rebuild the bridge and assured that it will be "even better than before."

Andrii Korezkyj of the Kyiv University of Transportation, however, says this is not likely to happen anytime soon. "All in all, I would estimate that it would take between one and a half and three years [to rebuild]," Korezkyj told DW, adding, "I don't think they'll let the Russians build another [bridge] arch like that near the Ukrainian border without interference."

Pro-Russian commentators, known as Z-bloggers, agree with this view. "Unlike in 2022 and 2023, the Ukrainian armed forces are now able to launch strikes against floating cranes and repair teams."

"Even installing a pre-assembled arch under these conditions would constitute a challenging task," writes the pro-Kremlin Rybar Telegram channel.

#104. Irishman charged with destroying US military aircraft with axe

The military plane, seen above, was effectively destroyed by the vandalism

US officials have charged an Irishman with destroying a $60m (Â£45m) American military aircraft after he allegedly sneaked onto the tarmac of an airport in Ireland.

Daithi O'Corrain, 48, is accused of doing extensive research before he allegedly used chemicals and various tools - including an axe - to damage a US Air National Guard C-130 Hercules parked at Shannon Airport near Limerick.

American prosecutors have charged him with destruction of government property, which carries a maximum sentence of 10 years in prison.

O'Corrain is not currently in US custody, according to the Associated Press news agency, and it is unclear whether he has a lawyer who could speak on his behalf.

In filings unsealed by prosecutors in New York on Tuesday, agents from the FBI argued that the act jeopardised US national security. They say the plane was parked at the airport while en route to another country on a US military assignment.

"As alleged, Daithi O'Corrain carried out a deliberate, planned attack against a US military aircraft, causing tens of millions of dollars in damage and jeopardizing critical military operations," prosecutors said in a court filing.

"This was not an act of spontaneous vandalism," said FBI Assistant Director in Charge James Barnacle Jr, explaining O'Corrain "spent months researching the aircraft, acquiring tools and materials, and preparing to maximize the destruction he could inflict."

Officials say that the suspect planned extensively for the 11 April 2026 raid, and brought tools with him for the demolition, which took place over several hours.

He entered the plane by breaking a locking mechanism, officials say, then used straps to close the doors before covering the windows.

"O'Corrain then proceeded to inflict severe damage to the interior of the Aircraft by, among other things, cutting wire bundles and hydraulic lines within the Aircraft, crimping a liquid oxygen line within the Aircraft, destroying every gauge in the Aircraft's cockpit, and puncturing the Aircraft's air ducts," the US indictment says.

He also used gallium, a substance that can erode aluminum and steel, ball bearings which he poured in the fuel tank, and an axe which he used to puncture the exterior of the plane.

He was caught by airport crew, and filmed standing on top of the plane. He was arrested at the scene, and charged by Ireland's national police with causing criminal damages.

O'Corrain seen standing on the rear of the aircraft in a handout from the US Department of Justice

The US charges say that he had planned well, because he avoided cutting a line that would have risked causing massive explosion.

According to the Limerick Post, external, O'Corrain is from Cork City. Supporters have described him as an anti-war activist.

Shannon Airport is a key hub for US military personnel, and is frequently the scene of anti-war protests. It was used by President Donald Trump in September for his visit to Ireland.

US prosecutors note that there have been several breaches there in recent years by protesters, including in May 2025 when three people drove a van through a perimeter fence, and then again later that month when a US airplane was sprayed with paint, making it temporarily inoperable.

#102. EU ‘to close door’ to talks on long-term UK ties until Burnham has decided on Brexit

Diplomats say piecemeal approach of resets is coming to an end and Europe wants clarity on whether Britain wants to reverse its exit

The EU will shut the door to discussions with Andy Burnham about the UK’s longer-term relationship with the bloc until the government has decided whether it wants to proceed with reversing Brexit, diplomats have said.

European capitals are also warning they have reached the end of the line for piecemeal “reset” summits with the UK and want the prime minister to focus on his next steps domestically before they proceed.

But they suggested a joint statement after a summit this Friday would signal that further discussions would follow on the UK’s entry to the EU’s energy single markets, irregular migration and a closer defence and security relationship.

“Clarity is what we need more than anything from the UK. Don’t come back until you know what you want. Work out what your options really are and then come to negotiate,” one EU diplomat said.

“The prime minister needs to bring the public with him. We can’t have another negotiation based on just half the country. We’ll only put the effort in if we know it’s not going to change down the line.”

Burnham is expected to set out his own next steps after the summit on Monday, including his hopes for a “national conversation” on what a closer relationship might look like.

With less than 48 hours before the Brussels summit, negotiators were still racing to finalise Friday’s agreement. A leaked draft seen by the Guardian shows continued disagreement over demands by the EU that the UK commit to aligning with the bloc’s trade defence measures, such as tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles, in exchange for inclusion in Made in Europe legislation.

The text shows the UK is pushing for a weaker commitment, only promising an “aim to protect supply chains consistently across our markets and guard against potential risks of circumvention”.

A Cabinet Office source said Made in Europe language was “the highest jeopardy” in the negotiations, while an EU official suggested it was the part of the discussions most likely to go “down to the wire” on Friday.

Though there is a formal commitment to annual reset summits, senior EU diplomats say the current approach, designed by Keir Starmer, will quickly “reach its limits” and more “mini deals” do not make sense.

One said the “piecemeal approach is coming to an end” and that Burnham’s intentions for the UK’s long-term relationship created a natural juncture for the EU too.

Another said: “It is like a buffet: you can choose to nibble on one or two dishes to start, but at a certain point you have to go for the whole of the buffet.”

One EU diplomat said there would be a “pause for reflection” after the Friday summit.

Burnham has said there is no rush to decide on the longer-term relationship, as he comes under pressure from within his own party to slow the pace of pursuing of closer ties.

He is not now expected to set out the full range of options for the future relationship – the status quo, customs union, single market or full rejoin – until he announces his 10-year plan in December.

Sources said the government was considering holding a year-long “national conversation” in 2027, including town halls and citizens assemblies, before deciding which route to pursue in advance of the next election.

However, the potential length of that debate has alarmed Brussels officials, who were caught off-guard by the launch of the new pro-Brexit campaign group Stay Out, set up by architects of the Vote Leave campaign.

One EU diplomat said: “The government would spend the year making evidence-based arguments about each of the four options, while Boris Johnson and Michael Gove and Nigel Farage will spend it shouting about traitors.”

After the reset deal is agreed on Friday, it will be enacted by the European partnership bill, covering the deals on food and agriculture, emissions trading and youth mobility. It is expected to be introduced before Christmas, with legislation laid possibly as soon as next week.

EU diplomats said the two sides were expected to settle on a quota of about 100,000 people a year being allowed to work, travel or study in each other’s territories. Originally, the UK had pitched for 50,000 and then 80,000, while Brussels has consistently sought a higher number.

Both sides have agreed that tuition fees for European students coming to the UK should be discussed at a later date, although there have been attempts by some member states to link the debate to the ratification of the whole reset deal.

Senior Labour figures are already beginning their push to have a full rejoining on the table. The former business secretary Peter Kyle said Burnham should fight the next election on a promise to rejoin the EU, with voters given a referendum on the outcome of any deal.

Kyle, who was a key member of Starmer’s cabinet, said the “only way through” the challenges of the domestic political debate was for the government to offer voters the choice to accept or reject any agreement.

He is the latest senior Labour MP to publicly wade into the Brexit debate, after the former foreign secretary David Lammy said the party should go into the next election clear that its intention was to rejoin.

Rachel Reeves, the former chancellor, has also said Britain’s future should be back in the bloc, arguing that leaving had made the UK “poorer, weaker and less secure”.

#101. Rubio says US not 'under systemic attack' after series of tech outages

Watch: Marco Rubio says recent tech outages are 'completely unrelated' to one another

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has dismissed widespread concerns online that the series of recent tech outages in the past week are connected, telling reporters there was "zero evidence" of any link.

Rubio acknowledged that "people are fearful" the spate of outages, which included all flights being grounded in the US and widespread disruptions to banking, Apple and Microsoft services, could be linked or part of a plot.

"We are not under systemic attack," he said. "These events, one has nothing to do with the other, they are not inter-related."

Cyber-security experts have agreed that there is no evidence linking the outages, and many of the issues appear due to updates to old infrastructure.

In the past week, there has been a massive Microsoft outage, an apparent tech glitch which grounded all US flights for hours, and issues for the National Security Agency, outages affecting the biggest US banks and Apple.

Every affected company or agency has said the issue was not cyber-related and not the work of an outside actor.

Rubio's comments came hours after US Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton also addressed the issue, telling Fox News the outages were not the product of a cyber-attack. He explained "lots of institutions" are patching or upgrading systems, which has caused some disruption.

Rubio was asked whether the Trump administration was concerned public fears could lead to stockpiling or a run on the banks.

He said the best way to combat such fears was for the US government to share everything they know with the American people.

"Every indicator we have is that every single one of these events has an explanation or a cause that has nothing to do with a cyber-criminal, has nothing to do with a hostile actor or another country," he said.

Technology-related disruptions have happened almost daily since Thursday, when a Microsoft outage disrupted many of its services, including email.

The next day, the US Federal Aviation Administration halted flights across the US for more than four hours with a rare national ground stop. It was the longest halting of flights since the 11 September 2001 attacks on the US.

The US transport secretary said the issue was technical and caused by a copper wire upgrade and outdated equipment.

On Monday, a United Airlines technology failure delayed about 150 flights and a JPMorgan Chase software problem disrupted service at the biggest and most utilised banks in the US.

Both United and Chase said a software issue was behind the glitch.

Apple users were impacted by outages affecting iCloud and Apple Pay on Tuesday. The company said the cause was not a cyber-attack but the outage was triggered by an an internal update.

The same day, a highly classified network used by the US National Security Agency (NSA) for internal communications also went offline for a period of time.

Passengers wait to check in at Los Angeles International Airport as flights resume after being grounded for several hours on November 13 due to a major communications outage.

Cybersecurity experts say there is no evidence connecting the Microsoft, airline, and bank disruptions from the last five days.

Kris Beevers, CEO and co-founder of the network and infrastructure management company NetBox Labs, acknowledged the circulating theories on social media linking the barrage of outages.

"There is no conspiracy here," he told the BBC.

"There is something more ordinary, and more important to understand: the infrastructure that runs the world is changing faster than ever, and change is risk. Much of it is 30 to 40 years old, with decades of technical debt, and it's now being asked to change very quickly."

Beevers said AI is only part of the story.

"It's putting more strain on these legacy networks, and it's also creating real urgency around security," he said.

"Our customers are the amazing teams behind these networks at airlines, banking, energy, and retail who are essentially doing heart surgery on a global scale to get these networks back up and running quickly."

Vice President of Threat Intelligence for Palo Alto Networks Unit 42, Sherrod DeGrippo, said: "What this week really underscores is how tightly coupled, and fragile, our core digital infrastructure is, where a single software bug or router failure can cascade nationwide."

#99. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#97. EU proposes €45bn loan for Ukraine to avert cash crisis and boost defences

Von der Leyen says she wants to give Kyiv ‘strong hand’ in talks and is confident all 27 member states will back idea

The EU executive has proposed a €45bn (£37.5bn) loan for Ukraine to help with a looming cash crisis and reinforce its defences, as Russia’s relentless bombardment continues.

Announcing the plan to lend Kyiv the money in 2027, the European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, said: “We think this is the best way to give Ukraine a strong hand at the negotiating table. And Ukraine only needs to repay once Russia pays for the damage it has caused in Ukraine.”

She expressed confidence all 27 member states would stand behind the loan, despite four central and eastern European countries having already ruled themselves out.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said he hoped for the swift adoption of the loan and was counting on the support of all EU member states: “Strong support for Ukraine is the best signal to Russia that this war must end,” he wrote on X.

Zelenskyy convinced EU leaders last month that his country faced a €70bn funding gap in 2027. The EU pledged to cover €45bn and wants Ukraine’s other allies to cover the rest.

Only 11 months ago, the EU agreed a €90bn loan for Ukraine, which was intended to cover two-thirds of Kyiv’s military and civilian needs in 2026 and 2027. But as Russia’s attacks have escalated, the cost of Ukraine’s defence and the toll of economic disruption continue to rise.

Andrius Kubilius, the EU defence commissioner, said Ukraine’s military spending was increasingly dominated by drones. Ukraine, he told reporters, expected Russia to use 10m drones this year and 20m in 2027. “Ukraine with EU assistance needs to match – and will match,” he said.

The EU loan is split into two parts: €25bn for weapons and €20bn for civilian needs, including funding essential services, such as schools and hospitals.

Von der Leyen expressed confidence that all member states would support the loan because “all 27 member states say very clearly that they want to support Ukraine”. She said member states could choose to support Ukraine’s civilian needs, rather than military spending.

Slovakia, Czechia, Hungary and Bulgaria, however, have said they do not intend to take part in the latest funding round for Ukraine, prompting a warning from the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, that he would block the EU’s seven-year budget unless all countries got onboard.

The four central and eastern countries are net beneficiaries of the EU budget, while Germany is the largest payer and Ukraine’s biggest EU donor.

The EU wants Ukraine’s non-EU allies – such as Norway, the UK and Canada – to find €25bn for Kyiv in 2027.

The EU proposal included provisions “to help other countries to help us”, a senior commission official said. Brussels has proposed that G7 countries that provide new loans get guarantees that the borrowing can be repaid with future profits from Russia’s frozen assets.

The profits from the Russian assets are being used to repay a €45bn loan issued by the G7 to Ukraine in 2024.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

Countries with a triple-A credit rating could opt to provide guarantees to the EU budget up to €5bn to expand European borrowing for Ukraine on capital markets. The commission has identified six countries that fall into this category: Australia, Canada, Liechtenstein, Norway, Singapore and Switzerland.

EU officials previously said the UK and other allies needed to do more to support Ukraine in 2027, arguing that the EU was shouldering too much of the burden. The UK subsequently pledged a £10bn economic and military support package for Ukraine.

Zelenskyy said: “The UK, Canada, Japan, and Norway have been helping tremendously in our defence of life, and establishing clear rules under which they can provide further aid in conjunction with the EU will help even more.”

Britain said on Wednesday night it would send 1,000 long-range attack drones to Ukraine as part of a further £260m military aid package, a day before prime minister Andy Burnham was due to travel to Berlin to co-chair a meeting of Kyiv’s European allies.

The commitment to support Ukraine’s deep strike campaign into Russia emerged a day after the US argued that Kyiv’s attacks on refineries and warehouses were ineffective and counter-productive, earning criticism from the UK and Germany.

“The UK will do everything we can to ensure Ukraine is able to defend itself,” Burnham said.

#96. Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years for Iran-backed plot to kill Trump

A Pakistani man with ties to Iran's government has been sentenced to 27 years in prison for his role in a plot to assassinate US President Donald Trump and other officials.

Asif Merchant was found guilty in March of attempting to commit an act of terrorism transcending national boundaries and murder for hire after trying to hire hitmen to kill Trump, former President Joe Biden, and former US ambassador to the United Nations Nikki Haley.

The hitmen were instead undercover FBI agents.

In court, he said he was acting under the direction of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) to avenge the 2020 US assassination of Qasem Soleimani, Iran's topmost military commander.

New York US federal judge Eric Ross Komitee sentenced Merchant on Tuesday to 330 months (27 years and six months) in prison, months after a jury handed its guilty verdict.

Although other US officials were potential targets, the assassination plot was largely focused on Trump because he was president at the time Soleimani was killed. Trump has spoken publicly about ordering the US airstrike that killed the Iranian general while he was travelling in Iraq.

In response to the killing, Iran's late Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei said "severe revenge awaits the criminals" behind the attack.

Prosecutors said Merchant initially travelled to Iran for religious pilgrimages and he was then introduced to an IRGC handler. He travelled to Iran 15 times where he received training from Iranian intelligence officers, prosecutors said, before flying to the US in April 2024 to carry out the plot.

Prosecutors said Merchant paid $5,000 to the undercover hitmen as part of an initial payment. He was arrested in July 2024 in Texas as he was about to leave the US.

In court, Merchant argued that he "had no other option" than to go along with the plot because his handler indicated that he knew who Merchant's Iranian relatives were and where they lived.

He said he had anticipated being arrested before anyone was killed and intended to cooperate with the US government, CBS, the BBC's US partner reported. He had hoped that would help him obtain a green card.

The jury deliberated for less than two hours before returning its verdict, US media said.

"An Iranian agent who came to the United States to coordinate an assassination attempt against President Trump has been justly sentenced for his criminal conduct," Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement.

"The Department of Justice will not tolerate foreign terrorist plots against our leaders and will not stop until those responsible are held accountable."

#94. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from all US freeways

Waymo has temporarily suspended its driverless vehicles from operating on all US freeways, the company has said.

The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless taxis from state freeways following a series of recent traffic incidents.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV) cited an "imminent hazard to public safety" for the ban, noting an incident this month in which several driverless Waymo vehicles entered an active construction zone on US Route 101.

Waymo said it was working with state officials "diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations", adding that no injuries or collisions were reported following the incident on US Route 101 south of San Francisco.

California's DMV said Waymo's driverless taxis presented a risk "due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present".

In another incident earlier this month, footage widely seen on social media appeared to show a Waymo car trying to overtake patrol vehicles that were deliberately slowing the pace of traffic on a San Francisco highway.

The California ban, which came into effect on 11 November, would remain in place until Waymo "demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions".

A Waymo spokesperson said: "In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

Data on Waymo's website suggests that its cars, compared to human drivers, are involved in 95% fewer crashes resulting in serious injury.

Waymo currently operates driverless taxis in 15 US cities, but only offered freeway rides in a handful of those locations, including San Francisco, Los Angeles and Miami.

The company's driverless taxis can continue to operate on regular city streets.

In May, Waymo recalled thousands of its self-driving cars in the US over a software issue that could allow vehicles to drive into flooded roads.

In the same month, multiple Waymo cars were spotted driving around a cul-de-sac in Atlanta, Georgia, in the early hours of the morning.

#91. ‘I didn’t notice his body being purple’: Byron Bay doula left newborn to inflate a jumping castle, court hears

Inquest into deaths of four babies born in free births or at home in northern NSW between 2022 and 2024 hears ‘something was seriously wrong’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Baby DH was born in an inflatable pool, in a candle-lit room in his parents’ home near Byron Bay, with no one medically trained present. There are two very different versions of what happened next.

In the parents’ version, the doula reassured them Baby DH was OK, then left to inflate a jumping castle while their newborn turned purple, became floppy, and struggled to breathe. In the doula’s version, everything seemed OK but she suggested they seek more help if they wanted to.

The New South Wales coroner’s court heard the two competing narratives on Wednesday as part of an ongoing inquest into the deaths of four babies born in free births or home births.

The babies died in NSW’s northern rivers region between 2022 and 2024.

“The evidence will be that from his first minutes of life, [Baby DH] showed signs that something was seriously wrong,” the Lismore courtroom heard.

“His parents saw those signs. They raised them again and again with their doula, who they had engaged to watch for exactly that. Their doula, in this case, was a woman named Amy Lou Smith.

“Each time the parents raised their concerns, they were reassured by Amy Lou. An ambulance was not called until moments before [Baby DH] stopped breathing.”

His father described the baby as floppy after birth. “His colour, his whole body was purple with his hands and feet darker still,” the court heard, and Smith told them some purple in the hands and feet was normal.

“I didn’t notice his body being purple,” Smith told the court.

Told the baby’s colour wasn’t improving, Smith said “as long as his abdomen isn’t purple”, according to evidence from the parents. The mother was shocked. His whole body had been purple all along. His temperature was low, 34.5C. Smith suggested trying to get it up to 37C.

“I believed if they had real concerns, that they would call an ambulance and not be texting a non medically trained doula,” Smith told the court.

Smith, the inquest heard, described his laboured breathing as “cute little noises”. “I didn’t say that,” Smith told the court.

The court heard Smith told the couple Baby DH would have been put on oxygen if he was in hospital. But Smith later told the court she didn’t know what the hospital would have done.

He had strange lumps on his body, and wasn’t feeding. Smith suggested they get the lumps checked out, and warned that babies sometimes take a while to latch on to the breast, according to the parents’ evidence.

The court heard Smith left to set up a jumping castle for her son’s birthday. She sent the couple a picture of the castle later, as they were trying to contact a doctor, the court heard.

Smith told the court the castle was already set up, and the couple was happy for her to leave.

“Everything felt like it was OK,” she said.

Eventually, the parents spoke to the GP they had tried to contact earlier. The GP told them to call triple zero. They did, and did CPR until the paramedics got there. But Baby DH was declared dead at the hospital.

After the father had made the emergency call, Smith also told them to call an ambulance, and sent them instructions on infant CPR.

Throughout the hearing, Smith denied that any of her advice or comments constituted clinical advice.

Neonatologist Dr Paul Evans, the court heard, had found Baby DH had a complex heart defect, one that would have shown up on an X-ray. The baby could have been treated, and a birth plan with proximity to a major hospital and specialist cardiac care prepared, the court heard.

The child’s death was not inevitable, Evans found.

While pregnant, Baby DH’s mother decided to rely on a doula. She first approached a woman the court heard was likely to be Sita Tara Kali, a self-described birth keeper who was also involved in the care of another baby previously mentioned during the inquest. But Kali didn’t feel she could help, so they turned to Smith.

The court heard the couple paid for an online “sovereign birth course” for Smith and was reassured that she had enough experience. They agreed on an emergency plan.

Smith, who became a birth doula in 2020, was quizzed on her experience in court.

She has a degree in fine art and has worked as a social worker and a youth worker.

She told the court she is not medically trained in any way but did an online “full-spectrum doula course”. She learned CPR but was not familiar with how to apply it to a newborn – she denied she had given the parents the impression she was.

She had no training about a newborn’s colour, breathing, or feeding, but she did go to a retreat where she learned about referring a woman to more support if needed.

She had been present at 15 births over four years, nine alongside a midwife, and conceded she didn’t know what a low-risk birth would look like. She had read a couple of books about birthing, but they were more “spiritual”, the court heard.

She agreed that she now believes the best model of care for a home birth is for a clinically trained midwife to work alongside a doula.

#90. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#88. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”
