#50. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#47. Jensen Huang to testify before Congress on ‘potential breach of national security’

High-stakes hearing comes in wake of resignation from Nvidia after alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia’s former CEO Jensen Huang will testify before several congressional committees on 1 December, setting the stage for a high-stakes hearing in the wake of his resignation following an affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

Tim Teter, Nvidia’s general counsel, has also agreed to appear for a closed-door transcribed interview the following day, 2 December.

“We look forward to hearing from Jensen Huang and Tim Teter on their roles in a potential breach of our national security,” Congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar and Rick Crawford, who chair the committees, said in a press release. “The American people need answers on Mr Huang and Nvidia’s role in allowing dangerous [People’s Republic of China] actors to access our most advanced chips, as well as the role of a potential Chinese intelligence asset in this scandal.”

The three congressmen chair the foreign affairs committee, the select committee on the Chinese Communist party, and the intelligence committee of the House of Representatives, respectively. On 12 November, they had signed a letter warning Huang that he could face a subpoena if he did not agree to the closed-door meeting.

Nvidia chips are key to powering the artificial intelligence boom. The Trump administration has prohibited the selling of advanced artificial intelligence chips to China.

Huang stepped down as Nvidia’s CEO after allegations of extramarital relationships with the suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang and two Nvidia employees surfaced.

Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, had been Megaspeed International’s executive director. The Singapore-based cloud computing company has bought nearly $5bn in AI chips and advanced hardware. The company said Alice Huang stepped down in 2025.

Megaspeed ultimately facilitated about $12bn in chip sales, in violation of US export controls. On Friday, Malaysia stated that they were investigating whether the company diverted advanced AI ships to other countries.

A recent joint Department of Commerce and Department of Justice letter, stated that the Department of Commerce, under its secretary, Howard Lutnick, first started an investigation in Megaspeed on 2024 for potential export control violations. The justice department, led by the attorney general, Todd Blanche, took over in 2025.

In his first remarks since his resignation, Jensen Huang said on Thursday he would be willing to return to Nvidia as CEO. Elon Musk and other Silicon Valley tech giants, along with 165,000 signatories to an open letter, have called for his reinstatement. Trump has also signaled his openness for the disgraced tech CEO’s return.

#45. Parents jailed for torture and death of four-year-old girl found along French motorway in 1987

The mother of a four-year-old girl killed in central France and found along a motorway almost 40 years ago has been sentenced to 20 years in prison.

Halima El Bakhti, 72, was found guilty on Tuesday of "torture and acts of barbarity resulting in death without the intent to kill", French media reports.

The girl's father, 74-year-old Ahmed Touloub, was handed a 15-year sentence as an accomplice.

Four-year-old Inass Touloub was found along the A10 road in SuÃ¨vres in the Loir-et-Cher department on 11 August 1987. Her body bore multiple marks of abuse, including human bite marks, broken bones and burns with an iron.

She was buried in an unmarked grave near the location where her body was found. Unidentified for three decades, she was known only as the "Little Martyr of the A10".

Then, in 2018, she was identified after the DNA of her brother â tested in an unrelated case â matched with hers, leading to the arrest of their parents.

The trial took place in Loir-et-Cher Assize Court from 2 November, with the verdict delivered on Tuesday after several hours of deliberation.

During the trial, prosecutor AnaÃ¯s Allio emphasised that "not a single part of Inass's body was spared", and recalled the "sense of vertigo that overcame us all" upon viewing the "unbearable photographs" from the autopsy, France Info reports.

The jury concluded that the child's mother was suffering from impaired judgement at the time of the girl's death, which reduced the maximum potential sentence.

French media outlet France Info described the two defendants as sitting stoically in chairs before the court with their heads bowed as the reasoning behind the verdict was read out.

El Bakhti and Touloub have 10 days to appeal the verdict.

Inass's death sparked one of France's biggest ever investigations, with the girl's photograph posted in public places and an alert sent to more than 30 countries.

#41. South Korea’s high-stakes university exams upended by AI cheating scandal

South Korea has been gripped by revelations of AI-aided cheating, just days before more than half a million students are to sit high-stakes entrance exams

The integrity of South Korea’s career-defining university entrance exam has been thrown into doubt after the top scorer in a recent professional exam admitted using a concealed camera and AI to cheat.

On Thursday, more than half a million people, mostly school students, are due to sit the exam, known as the suneung, at 1,312 centres across the country. The exam runs for a gruelling eight hours over a single day and determines which university a candidate can attend and, in turn, their future prospects.

The country goes to great lengths to ensure the smooth running of the suneung. Aircraft take-offs and landings are rescheduled during the 25-minute English listening paper to minimise noise, while artillery fire and tank movements are suspended.

The stock market opens an hour late and cars are stopped 200 metres from every centre. Extra trains run from 6am, police drive latecomers to their exam halls, and tourist attractions open late to ease traffic. Offices are asked to open at 10am.

But in recent days, exam organisers have faced a renewed threat to the validity of the entire exercise, after the top scorer in this year’s tax accountancy exam- who got just one question wrong out of 160– admitted on Sunday he had cheated by using AI.

Prime minister Han Seong-sook told a cabinet meeting in Seoul on Tuesday that AI-assisted cheating was a “social evil” that the government would “root out”.

“We will block outright the carrying of smart devices during the exam,” she said. The education ministry and other agencies should approach it “as if staking their jobs on it”, she added.

Han said smart devices must be turned off and handed in, while bags left in corridors would be checked for any devices that were still on. Exam officials would also inspect all candidates’ glasses and watches, as well as their collars, sleeves and buttons for concealed cameras.

The government will also send emergency alerts to every phone in the country, which sound at full volume and cannot be silenced, in the hope of catching out anyone who has not handed in their device.

More invigilators will be deployed, and the government said it was preparing to switch off school wifi inside exam centres and public wifi nearby, and that personal hotspots, which cannot be blocked, would be traced through connection records.

The weekend’s cheating revelation captured national headlines in South Korea, stoking concern about the security of the suneung.

In a video posted to his lawyer’s YouTube channel, the exam cheat said he had photographed the questions with a concealed microcamera, sent them to an AI system and read the answers from a smartphone. “I cheated in the tax accountant exam,” he said. “My top placing is not the result of legitimate effort.”

His lawyer handed police a camera, a laptop and a phone. No charges have been brought, and the prime minister has ordered procedures to begin that would void his result and bar him for five years.

The education minister, Choi Kyo-jin, told parliament on Monday that while authorities could screen out the smart glasses already on sale at Thursday’s exam, newer variants could not be dealt with perfectly.

Choi has ruled out postponing the exam, which has been delayed several times before, including in 2017, after an earthquake struck the south-eastern city of Pohang, and in 2020 due to the Covid pandemic.

Smart glasses have been banned in exams since the end of June. In October, several candidates were caught with them while sitting university-run admissions exams. At the suneung, wearing a pair counts as using one even when switched off.

The education ministry looked at using radio-frequency equipment to detect the glasses in exam halls, but concluded conditions varied too much between venues for deployment this year. Checks will instead rely on visual inspection, with at least one dedicated official placed in each exam centre.

Teachers’ unions have criticised the government response as belated. A survey last month of 2,716 secondary school teachers by the Korean Teachers and Education Workers Union showed that 82.3% said they could not tell whether a candidate was wearing smart glasses under the current procedure, with only 3.5% saying they could.

To avoid facing scrutiny on exam day, some candidates are reportedly switching to contact lenses. An optician in Suwon, south of Seoul, said students who had never worn contacts were coming in to ask how to put them in.

Han said on X on Monday that the checks would be handled carefully “so that candidates acting in good faith do not suffer unnecessary anxiety or inconvenience”.

In his video confession, the accountancy test cheat addressed suneung candidates directly. “The suneung is coming up soon,” he said.

“To anyone thinking of cheating as I did: I am going to tell the investigators everything, so please don’t think, ‘I won’t get caught.’”

→ Baca artikel asli

#40. Russian man accused of spying on US tech defence company that assists Ukraine

The alleged foreign agent was discovered with two Russian passports

US investigators have arrested a Russian man who they say was recruited by Moscow to spy on employees of a technology and defence firm in California's Silicon Valley that has links to Ukraine.

Igor D'iu is accused of acting as an agent of a foreign government to conduct a spying operation in Palo Alto, near San Francisco.

He is also accused of stalking employees of the company and possessing falsified identity documents.

The company is not named in charging documents, but is described as a publicly traded technology and defence company that produces software used by Ukraine in its war with Russia. US prosecutors say the company was targeted by Russia due to its assistance to Kyiv.

"The allegations in this case describe a state-sponsored operation to target and harass employees of an American technology and defense company because of that company's assistance to Ukraine in its war with Russia," said US Attorney for the Northern District of California Craig Missakian.

"We will not tolerate any efforts - by foreign governments or otherwise - to turn Silicon Valley into a base for foreign intelligence operations."

Prosecutors say that D'iu entered the US unlawfully via the Mexico border, after travelling there from Istanbul in June.

He was allegedly recruited through the Telegram encrypted messaging app. His handlers referred to Russia as "'the Motherland' and directed D'iu to gather information about the company because it was supplying weapons used against Russia in Ukraine".

On their instruction, he purchased computers, phones, wireless networking equipment and remote access software - tools he needed for his spy mission.

He then went to a coffee shop located near the company's offices in Palo Alto, where he photographed laptop screens of employees who were working there.

His handlers also allegedly "instructed him to position his laptop at designated locations near the company so they could remotely access it and attempt to collect information from company employees in the vicinity".

The suspect allegedly took photos of employees screens at a cafe near the office

According to CBS News, the BBC's US partner, the company in question is Palantir Technologies. Palantir did not reply to a BBC request for comment.

D'iu also allegedly conducted surveillance of employees and their residences.

On 3 September, he followed a vehicle driven by one senior executive for nearly 30 miles. He fled after she drove to a police station. Police later obtained evidence that he had followed other employees' cars on previous days.

During the investigation, officials discovered identity cards that had been falsified - including a US passport, California ID card, and a Social Security card with the fake name "Hugo Landry Johns".

A raid on his home in October in East Palo Alto turned up two Russian passports, and a handwritten notebook that contained numerous home addresses of employees "family members' names, educational and employment histories, and assessments of their strengths and weaknesses", prosecutors say.

He faces two charges related his alleged work as a foreign agent, one charge of interstate stalking, and one count of fraud related to his identity documents. He faces over 15 years in prison if found guilty.

The suspect was found with forged identification documents including a US passport

Ukraine's long-range drones using Western tech to hit Russia

He has been held in jail since 2 November. It is unclear whether he has hired a lawyer who can speak on his behalf.

The arrest comes as espionage allegations roil the US tech industry, amid the news that Nvidia ex-CEO Jensen Huang had a romantic affair with a woman suspected of spying for China.

Last month, a former OpenAI employee was charged with planting a listening device in a company office. The man was later accused of having "considerable support" from the Saudi government.

#36. Apec summit as it happened: Xi welcomes Trump on China visit as Putin claims Europe in ‘humiliating decline’

This blog is now closed, you can read more on this story here

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official. The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

Meanwhile, a meeting between the leaders of China, the United States and Russia planned for Thursday in Shenzhen is set to last less than 60 minutes, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told Russian state media on Wednesday. “No more than an hour” has been set aside for the historic summit between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, Peskov was quoted as saying by Russian state news agencies.

Xi Jinping told Donald Trump he hoped their talks would help advance a “constructive, strategic and stable relationship” between China and the US, based on “respect, fairness and reciprocity”, according to China’s state news agency Xinhua. Xi also recalled Trump’s personal welcome at the airport during his visit to the US in September, saying China’s own welcome for the US president reflected its tradition of reciprocity.

Xi personally greeted Trump on the airport tarmac in Shenzhen, reciprocating the welcome the US president extended to the Chinese leader during his visit to the US in September.

Before landing in Shenzhen, Donald Trump enjoyed lunch in Manila with Philippine president Ferdinand Marcos Jr at the Malacañang presidential palace. Speaking during a meeting ahead of lunch, Marcos revealed that he had arranged for fried chicken from Filipino fast food chain Jollibee to be delivered to the US delegation as a late-night snack last night, praising Trump for “working very hard”.

Chinese state media have released a readout of Xi Jinping’s earlier meeting with Vladimir Putin. In it, Xi is quoted as saying China-Russia relations are “unshaken by storms and steady as a mountain”, and have set “an example of a new type of major-country relationship characterised by equality, mutual respect, trustworthiness, and win-win co-operation”.

Canada’s Mark Carney has hit back at Vladimir Putin’s earlier assertion that sanctions no longer work in this day and age. “Mr Putin can ask his financial institutions, he can ask his oligarch friends, he can ask his hockey players whether or not sanctions are a thing of the past. I think he’ll find out that they’re very real, they’re biting,” Carney told reporters.

Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum won praise and went viral on Chinese social media on Wednesday for taking an economy flight to attend a regional summit in Shenzhen. World leaders this week descended on the southern Chinese city to attend the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meetings – many arriving in specially chartered commercial airliners or dedicated government aircraft like US president Donald Trump’s Air Force One.

It is unclear whether North Korean leader Kim Jong-un will attend the talks, but Trump has floated the possibility. On Wednesday US secretary of state Marco Rubio said the US president was willing to engage, but he did not confirm whether a meeting had been set in Shenzhen.

The issues put the administration on the defensive at a moment when the president wants to project strength and certainty before the meeting with Xi and Putin.

“It’s fine,” Trump said Tuesday from the Philippine capital of Manila, suggesting it was all just a consequence of being a nation that is “big and powerful.”

#33. Israeli military launches rare ground operation in Gaza beyond Yellow Line

Residents fled the area near Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camps following the Israeli evacuation order

Israeli ground forces have launched a rare operation in part of the central Gaza Strip outside their control, after issuing evacuation orders for residents.

Witnesses said tanks advanced east of Nuseirat refugee camp and rolled onto the territory's main north to south road under the cover of artillery strikes.

The Israeli military said the "targeted" operation aimed to dismantle a "terror tunnel" west of the so-called Yellow Line, which marks the more than 60% of Gaza's territory occupied by Israel.

The Palestinian armed group Hamas, which controls the rest of Gaza, accused Israel of a "flagrant violation" of the October 2025 ceasefire agreement.

Violence has continued in Gaza over the past 13 months, with near-daily Israeli strikes, and living conditions for the 2.1 million people there remain dire.

Early on Wednesday afternoon, Israeli military body Cogat's Arabic spokesperson announced the start of the incursion and warned Palestinians living in an area north of Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camp to stay away for their own safety.

It also said a section of the north to south Salah al-Din road that passed through the area would be closed to traffic.

Later, the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) said it had begun a joint operation with the Shin Bet security service "to dismantle a terror tunnel west of the Yellow Line that posed an immediate and direct threat to IDF troops".

"IDF troops under the Southern Command are deployed in the area in accordance with the ceasefire agreement and will continue to operate to remove any immediate threat and thwart attempts by the Hamas terrorist organisation to harm Israeli civilians or IDF troops, or to violate the agreement," it added.

Hamas warned in a statement that the advance of Israeli military vehicles beyond the Yellow Line and the evacuation of residents constituted "a dangerous development and a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement".

Footage published by Palestinian media outlets and on social media showed families fleeing areas near Nuseirat camp following the announcement.

In one video verified by BBC Verify, a man can be seen ducking after hearing artillery fire and then running along Salah al-Din road, about 1.5km (0.9 miles) west of the Yellow Line. "The Israeli occupation now is firing randomly near the houses and tents of people," he says.

Automatic gunfire can be heard in another video filmed just to the south-east.

A resident of Nuseirat who was forced to flee told BBC Arabic's Middle East Daily programme: "The situation is now extremely dire, with Israeli forces firing indiscriminately at areas where displaced people have sought shelter."

"We couldn't take any of our belongings with us - not even our blankets, pillows, or clothes. We had no choice but to leave everything behind."

A second resident also reported "indiscriminate gunfire and shelling" and said there had been "numerous casualties, including fatalities".

Asked about reported fatalities, the IDF sent a general statement about the operation.

Israel building new land barriers in Gaza and displacing families

Israel moves Yellow Line deeper into Gaza, satellite images show

Gaza's Hamas-run Civil Defence agency said three people were killed and others were wounded in three separate Israeli drone strikes on Wednesday.

One man was killed in Nuseirat, the second to the north in Gaza City, and the third in the central town of Deir al-Balah, it added.

The IDF told the BBC that it had "struck several military operatives".

At least 1,658 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza since the ceasefire began last year, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

The Israeli military says six of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Meanwhile, more than 1.9 million people - about 90% of Gaza's population - remain displaced, with 1.4 million of them living in tents or makeshift shelters, according to the UN.

Reconstruction has yet to begin in Gaza, where an estimated 82% of all structures have been destroyed or damaged during the war, as US President Donald Trump's peace plan has largely stalled.

The disarmament of Hamas has been a key sticking point.

The group has agreed to give up its weapons in exchange for a phased withdrawal of Israeli troops from the 60% of Gaza they currently occupy. But Israel has insisted that Hamas must fully disarm before any pullout.

The war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,700 people have been killed in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#30. Eight-year-old Haitian boy returns home after release from ICE custody

Child reunited with family after he was detained by immigration agents in San Diego and transferred to Texas

An eight-year-old Haitian boy who was put into restraints and detained by immigration agents in San Diego on 4 November and transferred to a facility in Texas has been reunited with his family in California.

Representative Juan Vargas shared a photograph of the boy and his family in a social media post on Monda, writing: “11 days ago, an 8-year-old in San Diego was taken into federal custody during an immigration check-in. He was separated from his loved ones and held in a facility in Texas. Today, he’s finally home.”

The Haitian Bridge Alliance, an immigration advocacy group, confirmed that the third-grader had been released from Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) custody.

“An eight-year-old child belongs in a classroom, on a playground, and surrounded by love – not in immigration detention. We welcome his release, but his freedom should never have required a public outcry,” Guerline Jozef, executive director of the Haitian Bridge Alliance, said in a statement.

The third-grader at Fay elementary school was put in restraints and taken into custody by federal immigration officers at a check-in earlier in the month, according to video obtained by NBC News and an oversight letter Democratic lawmakers sent to the Departments of Homeland Security and Health and Human Services.

A spokesperson for the homeland security department said ICE took the child into custody “out of an abundance of caution and to ensure the child’s safety”.

The uncle, the spokesperson said in a statement to the Guardian, was unable to prove legal guardianship – and that officials were obliged by federal anti-trafficking laws to take the boy into care as an “unaccompanied alien child”.

The Haitian Bridge Alliance said the boy was in the care of his uncle and cousins after his father was deported last year.

“I’m so grateful that he’s home, but this should have never happened in the first place,” Vargas said in a second social media post, adding that he would be asking the Trump administration for answers. While he was detained, the child was “held in a facility in Texas, far away from his home in San Diego”, Vargas said.

Celebrating the boy’s return, Jozef of the Haitian Bridge Alliance told NBC News: “The aunt was literally crying, and she said: ‘I have not eaten since the Wednesday they took my baby.’ They haven’t been able to sleep because of that.” She added: “The cousins were there when they took him, and wondering: will they ever see him again? But today [Monday] they are able to see him again. They are able to hug him.”

#28. Trump shakes hands with Putin and Xi ahead of historic talks in China

Watch: Moment Trump and Putin shake hands as historic trilateral meeting approaches

Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin met for the first time in more than a year on Wednesday, as they prepared for landmark talks in China with Xi Jinping.

The US president was greeted off his plane by the Chinese leader as he arrived in Shenzhen for a summit of Asia-Pacific leaders, which will culminate in a closely-watched meeting between the pair and the Russian president.

After a carefully choreographed handshake and hug between Trump and Xi on the tarmac, the US president later greeted and exchanged pleasantries with Putin ahead of a reception.

Trump and Putin are expected to hold one-on-one talks on the sidelines of the summit on Thursday, in which the war in Ukraine could be discussed.

However the main focus during the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum (Apec) will be on the US-China-Russia talks on Thursday - the first time the trio of leaders have met together in person.

A full agenda has not been made public but there have been various hints about what could be discussed, including the wars in Ukraine and Iran, nuclear proliferation, trade tensions and developments in artificial intelligence.

After a warm reception from Xi, the leaders of the US and Russia shook hands ahead of a reception

A lavish welcome ceremony was laid on for Trump's arrival

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said Putin and Trump have not yet held a substantive meeting, and merely greeted each other on Wednesday.

Peskov also said that "no more than an hour" had been allocated for Thursday's trilateral talks between Putin, Trump and Xi.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has raised fears of a plan to end the war being discussed in the absence of representatives from Kyiv or Europe.

It is unclear whether China would want this issue to be on the agenda in Shenzhen, with Beijing talking more generally about the need to discuss "safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability".

China has officially remained neutral since Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, rejecting accusations from Kyiv and its allies that it has been tacitly supporting Moscow, including by exporting dual-use technology used by the Russian military.

Wednesday's handshake was the first in-person meeting between Trump and Putin since August 2025, when the Russian leader travelled to Alaska for talks on the Ukraine war which failed to deliver a breakthrough.

Xi's personal reception for Trump at the airport mimicked a similar move by the US president when the Chinese leader visited Washington in September, a gesture Xi described as "special".

In Shenzhen, a red carpet was rolled out in front of Air Force One for Trump, with a cheering crowd, honour guard, military flyover and flowers presentation all in place to welcome him.

Chinese state-run news agency Xinhua quoted Xi as saying he wanted to pursue a "constructive strategic and stable relationship based on respect, fairness, and equality".

Xi's greeting will be seen as a diplomatic gesture designed to begin the week's talks with his American counterpart on a cordial note.

Watch: BBC correspondents discuss historic Trump, Xi and Putin meeting

The south-east Chinese city the Apec summit is being held in is a booming area for manufacturing and advanced tech.

It comes against the backdrop of the ousting of Nvidia chief executing Jensen Huang, who admitted to having an affair with a woman suspected by US intelligence of spying for Beijing - a claim dismissed as a "smear" by China.

Trump has pursued warmer relations with China but the incident points to ongoing tensions between the two powers.

Several hours before Trump's arrival, Putin landed in Shenzhen and held around two hours of talks with Xi, with whom he has pressed for closer economic and defence relations.

Xi said both countries would "move forward steadily and boldly" and that the relationship was "stable as a mountain, unshaken by storms".

#25. Isolated Brazilian tribe emerges amid alarm over threats to group’s way of life

Indigenous Awá people of Amazon make contact with villagers and accept food to take back to territory

About 20 Indigenous people emerged from isolation in Brazil’s Amazon rainforest, several organizations reported on Tuesday, raising the alarm about increased invasions into the tribe’s territory.

According to the NGO Survival International, members of the Awá people, who live in voluntary isolation, arrived on Monday in a village and made contact with a government team in a “historic encounter”.

The village they entered was within the Arariboia Indigenous territory in the north-eastern state of Maranhão, home to the Guajajara ethnic group.

Local community leaders said the villagers offered food to the group.

“They asked for yams and took them back into their forest,” said Survival International.

The NGO explained that the Awá typically subsist on hunting and gathering. However, they have faced increased incursions from illegal loggers, hunters and cattle ranchers.

A video of the encounter, filmed by Guajajara villagers and released by Survival International, shows a group of men, women and children at the edge of the forest.

Partially concealed by vegetation, they appear naked and are wearing necklaces. The men carry bows and long arrows.

“The physical condition of some members, particularly the children and the elderly, caused concern among the villagers, who noted clear signs of severe emaciation,” warned Coapima, an organization representing Indigenous groups in Maranhão.

Coapima highlighted the risks that unplanned contact poses to such groups, noting that they are particularly “exposed to diseases” against which they have little to no immunity.

Funai, the public agency responsible for Indigenous affairs, said in a statement sent to AFP that its technical teams were closely monitoring the situation and analyzing available information regarding the group’s health.

Survival International’s director, Caroline Pearce, said the group’s choice to emerge from isolation was a “troubling indication of how the continued invasion of uncontacted peoples’ forests puts otherwise self-sufficient Indigenous people in real danger”.

She said the Awá were being driven into “an ever-smaller island of the Amazon rainforest, surrounded by destruction”.

According to the NGO, about 196 “uncontacted” peoples and groups, who choose to live in isolation, have been identified across 10 countries.

More than 90% of such groups live in the Amazon rainforest, primarily in Brazil, with others in Indonesia and India.

The Forest Guardians – a group of Guajajara people who have battled illegal invasions for years – warned in comments in the Survival International statement that “climate change, fires, deforestation and land encroachment” were affecting the Awá’s way of life.

According to Survival International, many of the guardians have lost their lives trying to protect the territory they share with the Awá.

#23. Claude-maker Anthropic poised for $1.4tn stock market debut

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has quickly become one of the most notable names in the technology industry.

Anthropic is poised to hit a value of at least $1.4 trillion (Â£1.1 trillion) when it debuts on the public stock market.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the popular chatbot Claude said it will price shares at $83 each when it lists on Thursday on the Nasdaq, a tech-focused stock exchange based in New York.

That price will make it one of the most highly valued companies in the world.

It will be the first US company focused exclusively on AI to go public, marking a crucial test for the fast-growing industry.

The AI firm said earlier this month it would likely offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each, which valued the company at up to $1.3tn.

But demand for Anthropic's stock debut - commonly referred to as its initial public offering, or IPO - is very high, according to one early investor in the company who spoke with the BBC on condition of anonymity.

If that turns out to be the case, Anthropic's valuation could quickly blow past its target on Thursday when trading begins.

Depending on how strong that demand is, Anthropic could even exceed the early market valuation of Elon Musk's SpaceX, which was the world's most highly valued stock debut when it went public with a value of $2.2tn in June.

However, in the weeks that followed, SpaceX shares fell significantly in price.

As it stands, Anthropic's listing will see the firm raise more than $68bn from its initial investors. It is also expecting another $26bn from private placements. So the total amount the company will raise is expected to be $94bn.

The amount of cash Anthropic has raised from its listing is already more than SpaceX raised in June.

OpenAI, the maker of ChatGPT and Anthropic's biggest rival, is expected to go public next year.

Until then, Anthropic will be the only public company in the US that is considered a true AI company, or one that was founded and built around AI tools and products for consumers and businesses.

There is excitement around Anthropic's listing, but also some concern from investors around its ability to generate revenue and profits going forward.

Yang Wang, a principle financial analyst with Counterpoint Research, said the central issue for Anthropic is whether its business "can keep pace" as hundreds of billions of dollars in financial obligations come due in the next couple of years.

"Nevertheless, it is not an everyday occurrence for public investors to gain direct exposure to a frontier-model developer with the potential to shape the next stage of productivity," Wang said.

While nearly every other major tech company is pouring huge amounts of money into the technology and infrastructure required for their own AI tools, they are built around social media or online retail or online search. AI tools and applications are secondary.

"Anthropic going public gives us a whole new window into what AI demand looks like," Marta Norton, who heads investment strategy at investment firm Empower, told the BBC.

The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

Anthropic was started by a group of co-founders, led by siblings Dario Amodei and Daniela Amodei, all of whom left their work at OpenAI to start a new AI firm.

The company has carved itself out in the ongoing AI race as more focused on building the technology with safety top of mind.

It refused to comply with a demand by the US Department of Defense that Anthropic's tools be used in an essentially unrestricted way. And Dario Amodei has been outspoken in his belief that AI tools may soon be so powerful that they present a threat to human existence.

Nonetheless, the company is looking to grow quickly and gain more access to financing through the public markets in order to do so.

Norton said Anthropic going public now, in a time of market volatility comes down to the simple fact that the company needs access to more money.

"AI is financially demanding," Norton said. "The public markets open up the financial spigot a little bit more, and so the time has come."

But broader anxieties have started to grow about the AI industry's business model, which is built on expensive, reliant on in-demand computer chips, controversial data centres, and deals with other tech companies.

The Federal Reserve Bank has expressed concern about the rapid growth of AI-related debt being financed through "complex and opaque" structures, according to minutes from its October meeting to discuss a potential rate rise.

A few of the meeting's participants also noted that "AI-related exposures were becoming increasingly concentrated among a small number of firms".

Can Anthropic's record breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

#22. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting

#17. Hegseth uses Venezuela visit to boast of Maduro’s capture by US military

Defense secretary asserts US dominance on trip that would have seemed ‘impossible, almost unbelievable’ a year ago

Pete Hegseth has used his first visit to Venezuela since US special forces abducted its former president, Nicolás Maduro, to boast of the dictator’s capture during what he called “the single most sophisticated military operation in the history of Latin America”.

Maduro was controversially seized by Delta Force operatives in the early hours of 3 January, setting in motion an improbable rapprochement between Washington and Venezuela’s anti-imperialist “Chavista” regime, which has ruled since 1999.

Speaking outside the recently reopened US embassy in Venezuela’s capital, Caracas, on Wednesday, Hegseth said: “It’s a great honor to be here as the first United States secretary of war this century to set foot in Venezuela.​ Just one year ago my presence here … would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable.

“In fact, exactly one year ago today … [Maduro] stood on a stage not too far from here and yelled mockingly, ‘Come and get me!’” Hegseth recalled, before adding sarcastically: “Well, we answered that call.

“The reality is that … January 3 is the day that will go down in history as a day of liberation for Venezuela,” Hegseth claimed, although Maduro’s allies continue to hold power with US support.

Earlier in the day, Hegseth was welcomed to the Miraflores Palace by Maduro’s former vice-president, Delcy Rodríguez, who became acting president with Donald Trump’s blessing after his abduction.

In the aftermath of the deadly US attack, Hegseth was one of the bluntest Trump administration voices when it came to celebrating the invasion. “Maduro had his chance … he effed around and he found out,” the defense secretary bragged.

Speaking at the palace, Hegseth struck a more conciliatory tone as he vowed to deepen cooperation with Venezuela’s new leaders in brief comments that were broadcast by the state broadcaster VTV.

“I stand here on behalf of President Trump, who sends his greeting and looks forward to the opportunities, not just militarily … but also economically: the incredible resource capabilities that your country has, that we look forward to unlocking together with you,” Hegseth told Rodríguez and senior cabinet members, including the defense and foreign ministers, Gustavo González López and Oliver Blanco.

Hegseth said his visit to Rodríguez’s “beautiful country” was designed to re-establish and increase “defense cooperation” between “two great militaries” that less than a year ago were briefly at war. Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that Trump officials were trying to negotiate a long-term US troop presence in Venezuela as part of their push for regional hegemony.

“I appreciate your leadership and commitment to your country,” Hegseth told Maduro’s successor, a once outspoken anti-imperialist leftist who has overseen an unlikely realignment with Washington since her ally was grabbed.

During her five-minute address, Rodríguez, whose oil-rich country is widely seen to have become a US protectorate after January’s invasion, made no mention of Maduro, who is behind bars in New York City.

But referring indirectly to his capture and the subsequent reconciliation with Washington, she told Hegseth: “Thanks to the political and geopolitical maturity of our governments, we have – in a very short period of time, just a few months – turned what were very difficult and very tough times for our people, into an extraordinary space for cooperation and mutual benefit.”

Rodríguez hailed the “historic” energy deal her government recently struck with Trump, who has called it “the biggest oil deal in world history” as well as “the spoils” of his “conquest” of Venezuela.

Hegseth claimed the agreement, which reportedly involves 65bn barrels of reserves, “makes the strait of Hormuz a non-factor for the western hemisphere”.

Critics of Rodríguez’s political movement painted Hegseth’s visit as a humiliating act of genuflection for a president with no choice but to roll out the red carpet for the man whose troops captured her boss.

Writing on X, Luz Mely Reyes, a prominent Venezuelan journalist, said Hegseth’s visit was designed to underscore Washington’s “direct administration” of her country.

“Ten months after Nicolás Maduro’s capture, Washington no longer needs to fire shots to remind everyone who is in charge: the mere presence on Venezuelan soil of the architect of the January incursion reaffirms that the country’s operational sovereignty is subject to the White House’s directives,” Reyes said.

Reyes suspected the visit also carried a message for members of Venezuela’s armed forces. “Domestically, the trip forces the military high command to legitimize the man who directed the extraction of their former commander in chief,” she said. “In this regard, I wonder whether it might be a message of discipline intended to prevent any pockets of resistance in the barracks.”

Trump’s attempts to expand his Shield of the Americas anti-cartel coalition of Latin American governments suffered a major blow last month with the defeat of his far-right ally, Flávio Bolsonaro, in Brazil’s presidential election. The leftwing winner, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, has indicated his country will not join the initiative.

But Venezuela’s interim government is in little position to resist. During his surprise visit to Venezuela, Hegseth said Venezuela had gone from being “our most aggressive regional threat to [being] a compliant security partner”. He praised Rodríguez for working “closely, pragmatically, and diligently with the United States to stabilize Venezuela”. Hegseth claimed US and Venezuelan armed forces would work together to attack “narco-terrorists” operating on Venezuelan soil, vowing: “We will hunt you down. We will find you. And we will kill you.”

Concluding her speech at the palace, Rodríguez told her foe-turned-friend: “Thank you, secretary, and I wish you a lifetime of happiness.”

#16. Myanmar faces historic genocide verdict at UN court over Rohingya massacres

When the judges of the International Court of Justice read out their verdict in the seven-year-old genocide case filed by The Gambia against Myanmar on Thursday, they could make history.

In the years since the Genocide Convention was signed in 1948, after the Nazi Holocaust, dozens of individuals have been successfully prosecuted for the crime of genocide, in conflicts such as those in Rwanda and the former Yugoslavia.

But no state has ever been found guilty by an international court of committing genocide.

"It would be a judgment of landmark importance if The Gambia were to win this case," says Arsalan Suleman, the lawyer leading the case against Myanmar at the court. "It would give renewed vitality to the 1948 Genocide Convention and to the notion of international legal accountability for crimes committed by a state."

The mass expulsion of the Rohingya population of Rakhine State in August and September 2017 was without question a military operation of exceptional brutality.

I was among a very small number of journalists who witnessed the deliberate burning of Rohingya villages in Rakhine, and also the dreadful human suffering of the hundreds of thousands who fled to neighbouring Bangladesh.

Their accounts of killing, torture and mass rape by Burmese soldiers have been thoroughly documented by human rights groups and a United Nations fact-finding mission, external.

I have on many occasions interviewed ethnic Rakhine people about their animosity towards the Muslim population, and I have documented the systematic obliteration of what remained of Rohingya villages by the Myanmar state.

But the question posed at the UN's top court, the ICJ, is whether all of this amounted to genocide.

Hundreds of thousands of Rohingya fled across the Myanmar border in 2017 as attacks on them grew

The ICJ was established in 1945 to help settle disputes between states, not to try international crimes. However, it was chosen to address the attacks on Rohingyas in Myanmar because there were few other avenues of redress for the victims.

Myanmar authorities have denied accusations of ethnic cleansing or genocide, saying they were only responding to terrorist actions by Rohingya militants with so-called military "clearance operations".

A UN report in 2018, based on hundreds of interviews, said the army's tactics were "grossly disproportionate to actual security threats". It said top military figures in Myanmar must be investigated for genocide in Rakhine State and crimes against humanity in other areas. Myanmar rejected the report.

As Myanmar was not a signatory to the International Criminal Court, it was not possible to file a case there against the officials accused of atrocities in Rakhine State.

Instead in November 2019 The Gambia, backed by the Organisation of Islamic Co-operation, filed a case at the ICJ, arguing that all states are bound by the Genocide Convention, so all have an interest in upholding it.

It accused the state in Myanmar, not just the military, of genocide.

This was a novelty in international law; a state not directly affected by an act, arguing that it had legal standing to make a complaint because it was indirectly affected by any violations of such an important convention.

Myanmar objected to this argument, but the ICJ judges ruled that The Gambia was eligible to file the case.

Min Aung Hlaing, the general who now runs Myanmar, was responsible for the military's operations in 2017

The case drew even more attention when the Nobel laureate Aung San Suu Kyi, who at the time headed the elected government in Myanmar, chose to lead the initial legal defence of her country at The Hague in person, for which she was widely criticised.

She had very limited influence over the actions of the Myanmar military in Rakhine State, but her refusal to condemn them dismayed many of those who until then had admired her courage and adherence to democratic principles.

Since she was deposed by a coup in 2021 and placed under arrest the defence has been led by a new team appointed by the military.

Historically, proving that genocide has been committed has been very difficult. Judges apply a high bar to this.

Typically, where there is no explicitly genocidal programme, like that of the Nazis, the judges require that the evidence presented at court must be such that the only reasonable inference from the actions of the defendants is that they had genocidal intent.

When Bosnia-Herzegovina filed a genocide case against Serbia at the ICJ in 1993, it took the court 14 years to conclude that while incidents like the 1995 massacre at Srebrenica did amount to genocide, there was not sufficient proof that the state of Serbia had genocidal intent, though it was found to have failed to prevent genocide.

Myanmar's lawyers and Aung San Suu Kyi have argued something similar.

Suu Kyi said that The Gambia's team mischaracterised a complex conflict and military operation, and that while she accepted that war crimes may have been committed against the Rohingyas, that is not proof of genocidal intent.

Aung San Suu Kyi's refusal to condemn the Myanmar military's actions in Rakhine State dismayed her admirers

But Arsalan Suleman believes the weight of evidence his team gathered is enough to meet that high bar.

"What we presented to the court was the long history of discrimination targeting the Rohingya, and how that history, and the policies connected with it, provide a background to understand the government's language, rhetoric, propaganda, and policies that were part of the 2017 'clearance operations'," Suleman said.

"We discussed the 1982 citizenship law, and the tightening noose of discriminatory laws that restricted access to education, healthcare, livelihoods, and travel - all of which made life really impossible for the Rohingya and, we argued, was one of several indicators of genocidal intent."

What happens if the court does find in favour of The Gambia?

Myanmar would then find itself tarnished by having committed "the crime of crimes", as sitting on the same bench as Nazi Germany, the Rwandan Hutu militants and the Bosnian Serbs at Srebrenica.

That might seem a setback for Min Aung Hlaing, the general who now runs Myanmar, who above all was responsible for the military's operations in 2017, and who has been trying to improve his dismal international image in recent months.

There is no right of appeal against an ICJ verdict. However, there is also no penalty that follows an ICJ verdict.

The Burmese coup leader is focused mostly on rebuilding relations with neighbouring Asian countries, who are unlikely to pay too much attention to such a verdict, and much less on rebuilding relations with Western countries who do care.

Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh's Cox's Bazar mark the ninth anniversary of their exodus from Myanmar

But for the huge Rohingya diaspora this verdict matters a great deal.

"This is like formal recognition of our suffering," says Mohammed Nowkim, a Rohingya who was driven out of the Rakhine town of Buthidaung in 2017 and now lives in Canada.

"For the Rohingya, this trial is a moment of acknowledgement of our suffering after years of denial. The Rohingya have been waiting for justice for decades, not just this year."

He and other Rohingya activists hope that a ruling in favour of The Gambia will prompt more co-ordinated action by other countries to find a resolution which allows them to go home to Rakhine State, in safety.

More than a million are stuck in wretched camps in Bangladesh, barred from employment as international funding dries up.

But the obstacles to realising this hope are formidable.

Rakhine State has been ravaged by a conflict between the military and the insurgent Arakan Army, which now controls most of the territory. It is supported by the indigenous Rakhine population who are very hostile to the idea of the Rohingyas coming back.

Many Rohingya villages have been completely destroyed. And international support for them has faded over the years. Even an unprecedented genocide verdict at the world's highest court cannot change that reality.

Myanmar's army massacred Rohingyas. Now it wants their help

Seeing through the official story in Myanmar
